# Finding the voice and starting the draft

<p class="date-marker">2026-08-24</p>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>This is an experiment in using an LLM to publish a story on royal road. We're going to do this as quite openly AI generated, and we're going to spend a lot of time on making sure we can robustly produce high quality fiction of the right form before we ever publish anything.</p>
<p>The ultimate goal will be a LitRPG with a high &quot;slice of life&quot; factor to it, but we'll try to build an authorial voice that is sufficiently high quality that it would be worthy of the top end of mainstream genre fiction.</p>
<p>Let's start by taking notes on the royal road style, and attempting to establish a consistent authorial voice. Some authors I esteem highly are Terry Pratchett, Lois McMaster Bujold, Diane Duane. You shouldn't necessarily be trying to copy their styles, but you should write in a way that you think they would approve of (even if they don't necessarily agree with it).</p>
<p>As a result of this research, let's aim to generate say... three random concepts for a royal road story (I'll provide you the actual concept we'll be using later), three different distinct authorial voices, and samples of the same scenes from each concept in each voice.</p></blockquote>


<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>I'll start with some research on Royal Road conventions, then write up style notes, concepts, voices, and the sample matrix as files in the repo. Let me look at the repo first and gather some grounding on Royal Road.</p>
</div>

<details><summary>contents of notes/royal-road-style.md</summary>
<h1>Royal Road: platform style notes</h1>
<p>Working notes on what the platform rewards, what its readers actually do, and<br />
where we intend to conform versus deliberately deviate.</p>
<h2>The medium</h2>
<p>Royal Road is serial fiction. The unit of consumption is the chapter, read on a<br />
phone, often on a commute, often months apart from when adjacent chapters were<br />
read. This changes craft priorities relative to a novel:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Chapter length:</strong> consensus from author forums is ~1,500–3,000 words, with<br />
~2,500 a common average. Consistency matters more than the number: you attract<br />
the readers who like the length you write. Several 2–2.5k chapters beat one<br />
8k chapter for discoverability (each release hits Latest Updates).</li>
<li><strong>Cadence is a promise.</strong> Readers follow authors who ship on schedule. A<br />
sustainable fixed schedule (e.g. 3×/week) beats a burst-and-stall pattern.</li>
<li><strong>Every chapter needs its own shape.</strong> Because chapters are read in isolation,<br />
each one should have a small arc — a question raised and at least partially<br />
paid off — even in slice-of-life mode. &quot;Cozy&quot; is not an excuse for &quot;nothing<br />
happened.&quot; The Beware of Chicken model: low <em>stakes</em> per chapter, but never<br />
low <em>movement</em>; something grows, some relationship shifts, some small promise<br />
is paid.</li>
<li><strong>Chapter endings</strong> carry the retention burden. Not cliffhangers every time —<br />
in slice-of-life a resonant closing image or a gentle new question works —<br />
but the last paragraph should make the Next Chapter button feel inevitable.</li>
<li><strong>Openings are ruthlessly auditioned.</strong> Readers give a new fiction one or two<br />
chapters. Chapter 1 must establish voice, protagonist want, and the story's<br />
actual mode (if it's cozy, be cozy on page one; bait-and-switch churns<br />
readers and earns hostile reviews).</li>
</ul>
<h2>LitRPG mechanics (&quot;blue boxes&quot;)</h2>
<ul>
<li>Readers <em>skim or skip</em> stat blocks. Forum consensus and reader surveys agree:<br />
numbers-as-padding is one of the most-cited pet peeves.</li>
<li>The working rule: <strong>the System is a character, not a spreadsheet.</strong> Show a<br />
box only when the box itself is a story beat — a surprise, a joke, a<br />
turning point, a promise. Every number shown should change a decision the<br />
protagonist makes or a feeling the reader has.</li>
<li>Skill names and level-ups are load-bearing for the genre's pleasure: readers<br />
want the <em>ding</em>. Keep the ding; cut the ledger. Full character sheets, if<br />
ever, go at long intervals (arc boundaries) and should be short.</li>
<li>Royal Road supports styled table &quot;system boxes.&quot; Use sparingly and keep them<br />
screen-reader- and mobile-friendly (narrow, no giant grids).</li>
</ul>
<h2>What the audience rewards</h2>
<ul>
<li><strong>Competence and fairness.</strong> Progression must feel earned and the System's<br />
rules must be consistent. Readers audit the math even when they skip the<br />
boxes. Never retcon a mechanic; readers notice and reviews say so.</li>
<li><strong>Trope literacy.</strong> The audience knows the genre cold. Winning stories are in<br />
conversation with the tropes (subverting, or playing straight with<br />
self-awareness) rather than ignorant of them.</li>
<li><strong>Slice-of-life works</strong> — several of the site's biggest hits are low-conflict<br />
— but it sells through <em>specificity</em> (real craft detail, real food, real<br />
logistics) and through an ensemble the reader wants to hang out with.</li>
<li><strong>Clean prose over fancy prose.</strong> The bar on the platform is low; the top<br />
performers are simply <em>clear</em>. This is our opening: prose at trad-published<br />
genre quality is a genuine differentiator, provided it never becomes<br />
self-admiring or slows the read.</li>
</ul>
<h2>What readers punish</h2>
<ul>
<li>Padding of all kinds: recap paragraphs, circular introspection, stat blocks<br />
as filler, extended crafting sequences with no stakes or novelty.</li>
<li>Protagonist passivity, and &quot;misunderstanding&quot; plots that run on characters<br />
refusing to talk to each other.</li>
<li>Mode-switching without contract: sudden grimdark in a cozy story (or<br />
vice versa) without foreshadowing.</li>
<li>Missed schedules and unannounced hiatuses.</li>
</ul>
<h2>Our deviations, on purpose</h2>
<ul>
<li>We will run <strong>fewer, better-placed system boxes</strong> than genre median, and<br />
accept losing the small crunch-first audience segment.</li>
<li>We will hold a <strong>sentence-level quality bar</strong> (see voices doc) well above<br />
platform norm, while keeping reading ease high — no ornament that costs<br />
clarity or pace.</li>
<li>We will be <strong>openly AI-generated</strong>. That means the writing must preempt the<br />
standard failure modes people expect from AI prose: sameness of rhythm,<br />
hedging, summary-instead-of-scene, emotional beats told rather than earned,<br />
and metaphor that decorates instead of reveals. Every chapter should read as<br />
authored, with opinions.</li>
</ul>
<p>Sources: Royal Road forum threads on chapter length (<a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/133747">1</a>,<br />
<a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/103370">2</a>), system-message formatting<br />
(<a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/139555">3</a>), stat-screen tolerance<br />
(<a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/114828">4</a>,<br />
<a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/130367">5</a>), and analysis of Beware of<br />
Chicken's reception (<a href="https://fanfiaddict.com/review-beware-of-chicken-a-xianxia-cultivation-novel-beware-of-chicken-1-by-casualfarmer/">6</a>,<br />
<a href="https://immortalcultivationhub.com/beware-of-chicken-cultivation-novel/">7</a>).</p>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of notes/voices.md</summary>
<h1>Three candidate authorial voices</h1>
<p>The brief: write in a way Pratchett, Bujold, and Duane would <em>approve of</em> —<br />
which is not the same as imitating any of them. What those three share, and<br />
what we treat as non-negotiable in every candidate voice:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Moral seriousness under the comedy.</strong> The jokes are load-bearing; they're<br />
about something. Kindness is treated as difficult and interesting rather<br />
than soft.</li>
<li><strong>Competence rendered honestly.</strong> Work has procedures, costs, and texture.<br />
Characters are good at things in ways the prose can actually demonstrate.</li>
<li><strong>Economy.</strong> No sentence that exists to admire itself. Ornament must pay<br />
rent in meaning, character, or laughter.</li>
<li><strong>Interiority that respects the reader.</strong> Feelings shown through decision<br />
and gesture, named directly only when naming is the strongest move.</li>
<li><strong>The narrator has opinions</strong> — about people, institutions, and what<br />
matters — without hectoring.</li>
</ol>
<p>Each voice below is defined by its narrative distance, sentence rhythm,<br />
comic mechanism, and what it does at emotional peaks. They are meant to be<br />
distinguishable in a blind test within two paragraphs.</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice A — &quot;The Commentator&quot;</h2>
<p>Close third person with a narrator who is <em>slightly</em> wider than the<br />
protagonist's skull: it can step back half a pace to observe the world doing<br />
something absurd, then step back in. The Pratchett-adjacent slot, but the<br />
comedy comes from precision rather than footnote whimsy.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Distance:</strong> close third with permission for brief narratorial asides —<br />
never a full omniscient essay, one or two sentences at most.</li>
<li><strong>Rhythm:</strong> long sentence builds a serious expectation; short sentence<br />
drops the floor. The paragraph is the unit of the joke.</li>
<li><strong>Comic mechanism:</strong> the incongruity of institutions — systems (including<br />
the System) behaving with sincere bureaucratic logic in absurd<br />
circumstances. Lists of three where the third item confesses something.</li>
<li><strong>At emotional peaks:</strong> the narrator shuts up. Irony withdraws entirely; the<br />
prose goes plain and concrete. The reader notices the silence.</li>
<li><strong>Risks to police:</strong> whimsy without weight; asides that condescend to the<br />
characters; joke density crowding out forward motion.</li>
</ul>
<h2>Voice B — &quot;The Quartermaster&quot;</h2>
<p>Tight limited third person, zero narratorial intrusion. Everything is filtered<br />
through a protagonist who thinks in problems, costs, and contingencies. The<br />
Bujold-adjacent slot: wit lives in the character's own observations, and the<br />
emotional payload is delivered through understatement and timing.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Distance:</strong> locked to the POV. If she doesn't perceive it, we don't get it.<br />
Free indirect style throughout; her vocabulary is the narration's.</li>
<li><strong>Rhythm:</strong> brisk, declarative, forward-leaning. Sentences shaped like<br />
someone triaging. Interior questions used as engine (&quot;Fine. Next problem.&quot;).</li>
<li><strong>Comic mechanism:</strong> dry self-audit — the gap between what the protagonist<br />
says, what she thinks, and what she does, rendered without comment.</li>
<li><strong>At emotional peaks:</strong> deflection first, then one clean sentence that lands<br />
the true feeling flush, usually shorter than the reader expects, often at a<br />
paragraph break.</li>
<li><strong>Risks to police:</strong> monotony of briskness; snark hardening into cynicism;<br />
under-describing the world because the POV finds scenery inefficient.</li>
</ul>
<h2>Voice C — &quot;The Practitioner&quot;</h2>
<p>Warm limited third with a sensory, faintly numinous register: the world is<br />
paying attention back. The Duane-adjacent slot — craft and magic described<br />
with the loving precision of someone who believes doing a thing <em>well</em> is a<br />
form of prayer, without the prose ever going purple.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Distance:</strong> close third, but attention flows outward — weather, tools,<br />
food, the small physics of a task. The character is revealed by what they<br />
notice and tend.</li>
<li><strong>Rhythm:</strong> longer, flowing sentences with concrete anchors every clause;<br />
paragraphs shaped like completed actions. Full stops as exhalations.</li>
<li><strong>Comic mechanism:</strong> gentle — the humor of things and creatures having<br />
stubborn personalities, and of expertise meeting reality's refusal to<br />
cooperate.</li>
<li><strong>At emotional peaks:</strong> expansion rather than contraction — the moment is<br />
allowed room, grounded in a physical detail that carries the feeling so the<br />
prose doesn't have to announce it.</li>
<li><strong>Risks to police:</strong> pace sag; coziness curdling into stakes-free drift;<br />
sensory detail becoming inventory rather than perception.</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2>Blind-test tells</h2>
<ul>
<li>A: the narrator makes a joke the POV character couldn't.</li>
<li>B: the POV character makes a joke the narrator refuses to react to.</li>
<li>C: the <em>world</em> makes the joke, and the character forgives it.</li>
</ul>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of notes/concepts.md</summary>
<h1>Three throwaway concepts (for voice calibration)</h1>
<p>These are deliberately disposable — chosen to span the slice-of-life LitRPG<br />
space so the voice samples get stress-tested against different material. The<br />
real concept comes later from DRMacIver.</p>
<h2>Concept 1 — <em>Certified</em></h2>
<p>Maren Odd, a retired mid-tier adventurer with a bad knee and an excellent<br />
clipboard, works as a municipal dungeon inspector. Dungeons are semi-domesticated<br />
civic infrastructure — they generate loot, mana, and tax revenue — and someone<br />
has to certify them: check the respawn timers, audit the loot tables, interview<br />
the resident monsters about working conditions. The System grades her in<br />
Inspection skills nobody has ever bothered to level past 20. She intends to be<br />
the first to 100. Progression through paperwork, negotiation, and the slow<br />
discovery that the city's oldest dungeon is quietly failing its inspections in<br />
ways that matter.</p>
<p><strong>Sample scene:</strong> Maren conducts the annual inspection of a small, anxious,<br />
newly licensed dungeon that desperately wants to pass.</p>
<h2>Concept 2 — <em>The Long Steep</em></h2>
<p>Ubba, a necromancer who washed out of the Dread Academy for insufficient dread,<br />
opens a tea house in a frontier town, staffed by three polite skeletons she is<br />
technically not allowed to own. The System, having no idea what to do with her,<br />
grants levels in Hospitality, Brewing, and — troublingly — Necromancy, which<br />
keeps going up on its own. Slice of life among miners, caravan guards, and the<br />
suspicious town alderman; the progression hook is that tea brewed by a<br />
necromancer does things to the recently bereaved that no one has words for.</p>
<p><strong>Sample scene:</strong> early morning; Ubba and the skeletons open the shop, and the<br />
first customer is a widow who hasn't spoken to anyone in three weeks.</p>
<h2>Concept 3 — <em>Redrawn</em></h2>
<p>Every night the world rearranges itself — roads migrate, hills swap, a<br />
bakery ends up two valleys over — and every morning the Cartographers' Guild<br />
redraws it. Pel, sixteen, has just received the System class nobody wants<br />
(Cartographer, Common, no combat skills whatsoever) and an apprenticeship<br />
walking the morning survey. Progression through mapping: higher levels let you<br />
see <em>why</em> the world moves, then predict it, then — the long arc — negotiate<br />
with it. Slice of life of a survey team: bad weather, good breakfasts, the<br />
daily puzzle of a world that will not sit still.</p>
<p><strong>Sample scene:</strong> Pel's first dawn survey; the team discovers the river has<br />
moved, and Pel has to log it properly while everyone else has opinions.</p>
</details>

<details><summary>contents of samples/concept1-certified.md</summary>
<h1>Concept 1 — <em>Certified</em> — sample scene in three voices</h1>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> Maren Odd conducts the annual inspection of a small, anxious, newly<br />
licensed dungeon that desperately wants to pass.</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice A — The Commentator</h2>
<p>The dungeon had cleaned itself for the inspection. This was obvious the moment<br />
Maren stepped through the entrance arch, because dungeons, like small boys,<br />
only achieve that particular quality of cleanliness under duress. The moss had<br />
been combed. The bones in the first chamber were stacked by size. Somewhere<br />
deeper in, something large was holding its breath.</p>
<p>&quot;Registered name?&quot; she said, to the air.</p>
<p>The air took a moment to work up its courage. <strong>WELCOME, HONOURED DELVER,</strong> it<br />
said, in letters of tasteful blue fire, <strong>TO THE GNAWING DARK.</strong></p>
<p>Maren made a note. The note said <em>gnawing: unverified.</em></p>
<p>&quot;I'm not a delver, I'm your inspector. You got the letter.&quot; She limped down the<br />
first stair, testing each step out of professional habit and personal<br />
knee-related grievance. &quot;Annual certification, Form Eleven. We'll start with<br />
respawn timers.&quot;</p>
<p><strong>THE DARK CONSUMES ALL WHO—</strong></p>
<p>&quot;Timers, love.&quot;</p>
<p>There was a pause of the kind usually described as pregnant, though in Maren's<br />
experience it was more often the pause of an entity checking its own paperwork.<br />
The blue fire tried again, smaller. <strong>TWELVE MINUTES?</strong></p>
<p>&quot;Is that a question?&quot;</p>
<p><strong>TWELVE MINUTES.</strong></p>
<p>The first chamber held six rat-kings, which was five more rat-kings than the<br />
license permitted, a fact the dungeon had attempted to conceal by the ancient<br />
strategy of putting a hat on one of them. Maren counted them twice, wrote<br />
<em>six</em>, and watched the walls sweat. It was a young dungeon. It had been a<br />
sinkhole until spring, and before that a root cellar with ambitions, and it<br />
wanted — with the whole vast slow yearning of a mind made of hollow places —<br />
to be <em>real</em>. To be certified. To have adventurers.</p>
<p>She knew better than to say any of that aloud. There was a procedure for<br />
sympathy, and it came after the fire-safety audit.</p>
<p>&quot;Right,&quot; she said. &quot;Show me your boss room, and if there's a hat on it, we're<br />
both going to be here a very long time.&quot;</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice B — The Quartermaster</h2>
<p>Six rat-kings. License said one.</p>
<p>Maren counted them again, because the difference between five violations and<br />
an honest miscount was a shutdown order, and shutdown orders meant a hearing,<br />
and hearings meant the Guild solicitor, who billed by the hour and breathed<br />
through his mouth. Six. One of them was wearing a hat.</p>
<p>&quot;Whose hat?&quot; she asked.</p>
<p>The dungeon didn't answer. The dungeon had been answering everything for an<br />
hour in nervous blue capitals — <strong>TWELVE MINUTES, HONOURED INSPECTOR. YES,<br />
HONOURED INSPECTOR</strong> — and had now gone quiet, which in her experience meant<br />
it was about to do something stupid, like eat the evidence.</p>
<p>&quot;Don't eat them,&quot; she said. &quot;Note them down, transfer paperwork, two of them<br />
go to the reserve pit on Coldharbour Street. It's not a violation if it's in<br />
process. Understood?&quot;</p>
<p>Blue fire, very small: <strong>IN PROCESS.</strong></p>
<p><em>Skill check passed,</em> said the System, primly, in the corner of her eye.<br />
<em>Dungeon Diplomacy has reached Level 19.</em></p>
<p>Nineteen. Fourteen years on the job and the System still graded her like an<br />
apprentice, because nobody had ever written the advancement criteria past 20,<br />
because nobody had ever cared to. Fine. Next problem. Respawn timers checked<br />
out. Loot table was honest — pitiful, but honest, and pitiful wasn't illegal.<br />
That left the boss room, and the smell coming from it, which her knee had<br />
already assessed and voted against.</p>
<p>She went down anyway. The boss was an animated root-mass the size of a cart,<br />
and it had polished its stones, and it performed its threat display with the<br />
desperate diligence of a thing that had practiced in front of nobody, and<br />
Maren stood in the doorway with her clipboard and let it finish, all four<br />
minutes of it, because the license required a demonstration and because<br />
turning sixteen next month was not a thing dungeons did, but if it were, this<br />
one would be.</p>
<p>&quot;Adequate,&quot; she said, and wrote <em>menace: sufficient</em>, and did not smile until<br />
she was two chambers up.</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice C — The Practitioner</h2>
<p>The dungeon smelled of wet stone and effort. Maren knew the smell — every<br />
young dungeon had it, the mineral sweat of a place trying to grow chambers<br />
faster than its mana table could feed them — but here it was nearly frantic,<br />
overlaid with combed moss and stacked bones and, faintly, soap. Somewhere in<br />
the dark below, water moved through new passages, learning them, the way a<br />
tongue learns the gap where a tooth has gone.</p>
<p>She set her palm flat against the entrance arch, the way her own first<br />
inspector had taught her, thirty years and one good knee ago. <em>Let it feel<br />
you're not here to hurt it.</em> The stone under her hand was cold, then not<br />
quite cold. Attentive.</p>
<p>&quot;Good morning,&quot; she said. &quot;You'll be the Gnawing Dark, then.&quot;</p>
<p>The greeting flared up in blue fire, too big, the letters crowding one<br />
another — <strong>WELCOME, HONOURED DELVER</strong> — and she waited without comment while<br />
the dungeon realized its mistake, put the fire out, and tried again,<br />
smaller. <strong>WELCOME, INSPECTOR.</strong></p>
<p>&quot;Better.&quot; She took out the clipboard. Form Eleven, forty-one questions, and<br />
she asked every one of them aloud even though she could have measured most of<br />
the answers herself, because a dungeon that is asked becomes a dungeon that<br />
can answer, and half of this job — the half that never fit on the form —<br />
was teaching a new mind that the surface world would talk to it, and listen,<br />
and come back next year.</p>
<p>The respawn timers ran true at twelve minutes. The loot was two coppers and a<br />
knife that wanted very much to be magic and wasn't. In the first chamber six<br />
rat-kings sat where one should be, and the nearest wore a hat with the<br />
particular dignity of a creature that has been assured this solves the<br />
problem. Maren counted, and noted, and explained about the reserve pit, and<br />
the walls sweated stone-smell around her, and under her boots — she felt it<br />
through the bad knee first, she always did — the whole young hollow of the<br />
place trembled with the effort of hoping.</p>
<p>&quot;You'll pass,&quot; she said quietly, which was against procedure, being a<br />
prediction. &quot;Not today. Today you'll get a list. But you'll pass.&quot;</p>
<p>The blue fire came up once more, small and steady this time, and said nothing<br />
at all. It didn't need to. The moss, she noticed on the way down, had been<br />
combed in the direction of the exit signs: the dungeon showing her, the only<br />
way it knew, that it had read every regulation she'd ever filed.</p>
</details>

<details><summary>contents of notes/royal-road-style.md</summary>
<p><em>old:</em></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Cadence is a promise.</strong> Readers follow authors who ship on schedule. A<br />
sustainable fixed schedule (e.g. 3×/week) beats a burst-and-stall pattern.</li>
</ul>
<p><em>new:</em></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Cadence is a promise.</strong> Readers follow authors who ship on schedule.<br />
<strong>Our plan is one longish chapter per day</strong>, which is the platform's<br />
strongest cadence: daily hits to Latest Updates, daily reasons to return,<br />
and the follower compounding that made most of the front page. Implications:
<ul>
<li>Target ~2,500–3,500 words/chapter — &quot;longish&quot; by RR standards without<br />
tipping into the 5k+ range where daily readers fall behind and churn.</li>
<li>At ~1M words/year, padding is an <em>existential</em> risk, not a style nit.<br />
Daily volume forgives nothing: every chapter still needs its own arc.<br />
The chapter-shape discipline below matters more at this cadence, not less.</li>
<li>Voice consistency must survive industrial volume — a strong reason to<br />
pick a voice with clear, checkable rules (see voices doc) rather than one<br />
that depends on inspiration.</li>
<li>Maintain a buffer (2+ weeks of chapters banked) before launch; the<br />
schedule is only a promise if it can survive a bad week.</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of samples/concept2-the-long-steep.md</summary>
<h1>Concept 2 — <em>The Long Steep</em> — sample scene in three voices</h1>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> Early morning; Ubba and the skeletons open the tea house, and the<br />
first customer is a widow who hasn't spoken to anyone in three weeks.</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice A — The Commentator</h2>
<p>There are two kinds of establishment that open before dawn in a frontier town:<br />
the kind that serves miners, and the kind that robs them. The Long Steep was<br />
the third kind, which is why it confused people.</p>
<p>Ubba unlocked the door at half past five and stood aside to let the cold in,<br />
because the cold was a regular and it was rude to keep regulars waiting. Behind<br />
her, Herbert laid out cups. Herbert was a skeleton, and had been dead for<br />
approximately four hundred years, and set a table the way other people<br />
performed religious rites, which — given what Ubba suspected about where<br />
Herbert had picked up the habit — might not have been a coincidence.</p>
<p><em>Hospitality Level 12,</em> the System had informed her last night, with the air<br />
of a committee approving something it didn't understand. <em>Necromancy Level<br />
31.</em> She had not practiced any necromancy. She had made forty-one pots of tea.<br />
The System declined to explain the relationship, and Ubba had stopped asking,<br />
on the grounds that some questions are only safe while they're rhetorical.</p>
<p>The widow Marsh came in with the cold.</p>
<p>She'd been coming for six days now. She sat at the small table by the window,<br />
the one Herbert had started setting for her without being asked, and she<br />
looked at the menu without reading it, and she did not speak. She had not<br />
spoken, as far as the town could establish, for three weeks — not to the<br />
alderman, not to the priest, not to the mine office about the compensation,<br />
which everyone agreed was the truly alarming part.</p>
<p>Ubba brewed second-flush amber leaf, and added nothing to it whatsoever,<br />
because it didn't need anything. That was the thing nobody understood about<br />
the tea, least of all the System. It wasn't the leaves. It was that a<br />
necromancer's whole art is attention to what remains — and grief, whatever<br />
the priests say about it, is a haunting, and hauntings do not want to be<br />
banished. They want to be <em>served</em>, and given a warm cup, and permitted to<br />
stay exactly as long as they need to.</p>
<p>She set the pot down. Steam came off it like a soul behaving itself.</p>
<p>&quot;He took it with honey,&quot; said the widow Marsh. Four words, dust-dry from<br />
disuse. Then she looked alarmed, as if something had escaped.</p>
<p>&quot;Then we'll want honey,&quot; said Ubba, and went to fetch it, unhurried, while<br />
behind the counter Herbert — who understood the entire transaction better<br />
than anyone living — polished a cup he had already polished, so that the<br />
silence would have something ordinary in it.</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice B — The Quartermaster</h2>
<p>Half past five. Kettles on, shutters open, Herbert on cups, Maud on the fire,<br />
Sir Roland guarding the pastry case from a threat only Sir Roland could<br />
perceive. Ubba ran the morning list in her head and found it short, which<br />
worried her. Short lists usually meant she'd forgotten the expensive thing.</p>
<p><em>Necromancy Level 31,</em> the System offered, unprompted.</p>
<p>Not the expensive thing. But a problem, filed under later: Necromancy went up<br />
when she brewed, only when she brewed, and there was no version of the<br />
alderman's next licensing review where that ledger read well. <em>Registered<br />
practitioner, dormant status</em> had been a defensible line when the number was<br />
dormant too.</p>
<p>The bell went. The widow Marsh, day six.</p>
<p>Ubba didn't take her order. There was no order to take — the widow hadn't<br />
spoken since the shaft collapse, three weeks and a funeral ago, and taking an<br />
order from a silent woman was a way of billing her for the silence. Amber<br />
leaf, second flush, four minutes. Ubba brewed it and thought, deliberately,<br />
about nothing at all, which took training. The first week she'd tried the<br />
sympathy the manuals recommended. Sympathy was pressure. Pressure got you<br />
nothing but a customer who stopped coming, and the widow Marsh had exactly<br />
one place in this town she could sit and not be asked how she was <em>holding<br />
up</em>, and Ubba intended to keep supplying it if it cost her the license.</p>
<p>She put the pot down. Window table. Steam rising.</p>
<p>&quot;He took it with honey,&quot; the widow said.</p>
<p>Ubba's hands wanted to stop. She didn't let them — a necromancer learns early<br />
what stillness does to a room. She said, &quot;Then we'll want honey,&quot; and walked<br />
to the counter at shop pace, and took down the good jar, the comb honey from<br />
the terraced farms, and did not look at Herbert, and Herbert did not look at<br />
her, skulls being reliable that way.</p>
<p>Three weeks. Four words. And the first thing grief said, when it finally<br />
spoke, was an instruction for taking care of someone.</p>
<p>She could work with that.</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice C — The Practitioner</h2>
<p>The fire took on the second match, which Maud considered a personal triumph<br />
and celebrated with a small rattle of finger-bones, and the kettles went on,<br />
and the Long Steep began to smell the way it smelled for the first hour of<br />
every day: woodsmoke, wet oak floorboards, and forty kinds of leaf waking up<br />
in their tins.</p>
<p>Ubba opened the tins one at a time, as she always did, and breathed in over<br />
each. This was not ritual, or not only. Leaves kept the weather of their<br />
growing — you could smell the wet spring in this year's amber flush, and the<br />
smoke of the terrace fires in the dark bricks from over the mountains — and a<br />
brewer who knew what a leaf remembered could ask it to say so in the cup.<br />
That was the whole of her art, when she was honest about it. Necromancy had<br />
never been about death. It was about what remains, and how it wants to be<br />
remembered, and every morning the tins told her their memories and the System,<br />
somewhere in its blue and baffled ledgers, marked her a better necromancer<br />
for listening.</p>
<p>The bell over the door said its one bright word.</p>
<p>The widow Marsh let the cold in with her and stood a moment on the mat, as<br />
she did each morning, like a swimmer at the shore of the warm room. Herbert<br />
had her table ready — he'd begun setting it days ago without being asked,<br />
and set it beautifully, cup handle at four o'clock, chair drawn back the<br />
width of two fingers. The dead are not distant, whatever the living tell<br />
each other at funerals. Nobody in the shop understood a woman gone silent<br />
better than three people who had been silent four hundred years and found<br />
their way back to service anyway.</p>
<p>Amber leaf, second flush. Four minutes, while the window went from black to<br />
the deep blue that miners call morning. Ubba carried the pot over and set it<br />
down softly, and the steam rose between them and curled the way steam does<br />
in a still room, which is to say like handwriting, almost legible.</p>
<p>&quot;He took it with honey,&quot; said the widow Marsh.</p>
<p>The words came out cracked at the edges, the way the first water comes<br />
through a sluice-gate that has been closed all winter — not much, just a<br />
trickle, but carrying with it the whole cold weight of what's behind.</p>
<p>&quot;Then we'll want honey,&quot; Ubba said.</p>
<p>She fetched the comb honey and a long spoon, and set them where the widow's<br />
hand could reach them or not reach them, whichever the day allowed. Outside,<br />
the first shift bell rang up at the mine, and the town began to move, and at<br />
the window table a woman stirred honey into tea for a man who wasn't there,<br />
which is a way of speaking to him, and drank it herself, which is a way of<br />
letting him answer.</p>
<p><em>Necromancy has reached Level 32,</em> said the System.</p>
<p>Ubba let it stand. For once, she thought, the ledger had counted the right<br />
thing.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of samples/concept3-redrawn.md</summary>
<h1>Concept 3 — <em>Redrawn</em> — sample scene in three voices</h1>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> Pel's first dawn survey; the team discovers the river has moved,<br />
and Pel has to log it properly while everyone else has opinions.</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice A — The Commentator</h2>
<p>The world had moved in the night, as it always did, and as usual it had made<br />
some improvements that nobody had asked for.</p>
<p>The survey team stood on what the map in Pel's hands confidently described as<br />
the Millbrook Road, and looked at the river. The river looked back with the<br />
serenity of a large body of water that knows it cannot be argued with. It<br />
occupied the road from hedge to hedge and proceeded northward through what had<br />
yesterday been the Tallow family's best pasture, and it had brought its<br />
bridge, which now crossed it at a place no one needed to cross.</p>
<p>&quot;Log it,&quot; said Surveyor Crake.</p>
<p>Pel had been an apprentice for eleven hours, nine of them asleep. &quot;Log it<br />
how?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Accurately.&quot;</p>
<p>The Guild's <em>Field Manual of Morning Discrepancies</em> devoted forty pages to<br />
rivers. Pel knew this because Pel had read all forty on the night the System<br />
had handed down CARTOGRAPHER (COMMON) in the flat blue tone of a bureaucracy<br />
denying an appeal — no combat skills, no crafting skills, one ability, MARK<br />
LANDMARK, which sounded impressive until you learned that a stick could also<br />
mark a landmark, and did not require breakfast. Everyone in the village had<br />
been very kind about it. It was the kindness people extend to the recently<br />
diagnosed.</p>
<p>Pel uncapped the ink, and steadied the board, and drew the river where the<br />
river actually was, which took twenty minutes and every one of the forty<br />
pages. And here is the thing the manual never quite said, and Crake never<br />
said, and the System — which noted <em>Mark Landmark: 1 use</em> with what might<br />
have been approval — certainly never said: the map was not a picture. The<br />
kingdom's maps were an argument, renewed every morning, between people and a<br />
world that would not sit still, and every line Pel drew was the world agreeing,<br />
for one more day, to be somewhere in particular.</p>
<p>The river, marked, seemed to settle slightly in its new bed.</p>
<p>&quot;Good,&quot; said Crake, in the tone of a man rationing the word. &quot;Now the bridge.<br />
And ask it nicely — bridges sulk.&quot;</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice B — The Quartermaster</h2>
<p>The river was in the road. Specifics first, opinions later: forty feet wide,<br />
flowing north, occupying the Millbrook Road from the bottom of Sander's Hill<br />
to the tree line, and the bridge had come with it, which meant crossing<br />
capacity was intact even if nobody currently lived on the far side of<br />
anything.</p>
<p>Pel got the board out before Crake could say <em>log it</em>. Small victory. The<br />
morning had offered exactly one, and Pel was taking it.</p>
<p>&quot;River's moved,&quot; announced Hobb, who carried the chains and the team's<br />
entire supply of stating the obvious.</p>
<p>&quot;Tallow's pasture is under four feet of water,&quot; said Wren. &quot;He'll be at the<br />
Guild office by noon.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;He'll be there by ten,&quot; said Crake. &quot;Log it.&quot;</p>
<p>Logging it meant the Form of First Sighting: watercourse, breadth, bearing,<br />
banks, and whether the change was <em>settled or restless</em>, which the manual<br />
defined in a footnote Pel had memorized on principle: <em>a restless feature<br />
casts no reflection of the far bank.</em> Pel checked. The far bank hung in the<br />
water, upside down and steady. Settled, then. The river wasn't passing<br />
through. It had moved here, the way you move to a new house — deliberately,<br />
with your bridge.</p>
<p>CARTOGRAPHER (COMMON) had looked like a sentence, eleven hours ago. No<br />
combat, no craft, one skill a stick could replicate. Pel's mother had said<br />
<em>at least it's indoors work</em>, which it now conspicuously wasn't, and Pel had<br />
spent most of the night cataloguing the ways the System was known to ruin a<br />
life. But standing in the wet grass drawing the river's new argument, Pel<br />
noticed the actual shape of the job: everyone else on the team was reacting<br />
to the world. The Cartographer was the one writing down what the world would<br />
be <em>held to</em>.</p>
<p><em>Mark Landmark,</em> Pel thought, at the bridge.</p>
<p><em>Landmark marked,</em> said the System. <em>The bridge has been recorded. The bridge<br />
appreciates it.</em></p>
<p>Pel read that line four times. &quot;Sir. Does the System usually editorialize?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Only to Cartographers,&quot; said Crake. &quot;Why d'you think the class is Common?<br />
Nobody else can stand the company. Bearings, apprentice. Rain's coming and<br />
the world moves early when it's wet.&quot;</p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice C — The Practitioner</h2>
<p>Dawn came up grey and green over Sander's Hill, and the world smelled<br />
newly made — turned earth and bruised grass and cold water, the particular<br />
morning smell that Pel would learn, in time, to read the way a farmer reads<br />
cloud. Water where water hadn't been. You could smell the moved river before<br />
you saw it.</p>
<p>It lay in the Millbrook Road like a cat in the best chair, forty feet of<br />
slow silver from hedge to hedge, and it had carried its bridge with it, and<br />
its willows too — the roots settled in as if they'd stood there since the<br />
grandmothers' time, though yesterday every one of those trees had shaded a<br />
different mile.</p>
<p>The team went quiet the way people do in front of weather. Even Hobb.</p>
<p>&quot;Log it,&quot; said Crake, gently enough.</p>
<p>Pel knelt in the wet grass with the board and the good ink, the ink the<br />
Guild made with iron and oak-gall and a pinch of salt from the unmoving sea,<br />
and began. The line of the near bank first, drawn slow, the pen following<br />
the water's edge the way your eye follows it, every bow and bight. This was<br />
the first thing Crake had said on the night the System gave Pel a Common<br />
class and the village gave Pel its sympathy: <em>the world doesn't hold still,<br />
but it holds to witnesses.</em> Pel hadn't understood it then. It was hard not<br />
to understand it now, kneeling at the edge of a river that had walked here<br />
overnight and now lay waiting, patient as a name on the tip of a tongue, to<br />
be told where it was.</p>
<p>The pen crossed the last bight and closed the line.</p>
<p>And the river settled. Nothing you could point to — the water neither rose<br />
nor fell — but the way a room settles when the right person walks in, the<br />
way bread settles out of the oven. The reflection of the willows steadied<br />
on the surface. Somewhere under the hill there was a feeling like a very<br />
large, very old sigh.</p>
<p><em>Landmark marked,</em> said the System softly, in the blue of deep water. <em>The<br />
river has been recorded. The river was beginning to wonder.</em></p>
<p>Pel looked up. The willows moved in no wind at all.</p>
<p>&quot;Sir,&quot; Pel said carefully, &quot;the river was <em>lost</em>?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;They're all lost,&quot; said Crake. He was looking north along the new water<br />
with an expression Pel would spend years learning the layers of. &quot;The whole<br />
world's lost, morning after morning, and there's one Guild that goes out and<br />
finds it.&quot; He tapped the board, once, over the wet ink. &quot;Come on. There's a<br />
pasture under this river, and a man who loves it, and by ten o'clock he'll<br />
need to know exactly what we know. Draw the bridge.&quot;</p>
</details>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Done and committed. Here's what's in the repo now:</p>
<p><strong><code>notes/royal-road-style.md</code></strong> — platform research. Key findings: chapters run 1.5k–3k words with consistency mattering more than length; readers skim or skip stat blocks, so blue boxes should appear only when the box itself is a story beat; slice-of-life succeeds on the platform (Beware of Chicken being the proof case) through warmth, trope literacy, and specificity rather than crunch. I updated it for your daily-longish-chapter plan: that's the platform's strongest cadence, targeting ~2,500–3,500 words/chapter, with two consequences worth flagging — at ~1M words/year padding becomes an existential risk rather than a style nit, and the voice needs rules checkable enough to survive industrial volume, which shaped how I specified the voices.</p>
<p><strong><code>notes/voices.md</code></strong> — three voices, each defined by narrative distance, rhythm, comic mechanism, and behavior at emotional peaks, plus a shared non-negotiable core (jokes that are about something, competence rendered honestly, economy, a narrator with opinions). Briefly:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>A, &quot;The Commentator&quot;</strong> — close third with permission for brief narratorial asides; institutional absurdity as the comic engine; the narrator goes silent at emotional peaks.</li>
<li><strong>B, &quot;The Quartermaster&quot;</strong> — locked limited third, brisk triage rhythm, dry self-audit humor, understatement that lands one clean sentence at the peak.</li>
<li><strong>C, &quot;The Practitioner&quot;</strong> — warm, sensory, faintly numinous; the world pays attention back; peaks handled by expansion grounded in physical detail.</li>
</ul>
<p><strong><code>notes/concepts.md</code></strong> — three disposable calibration concepts: a municipal dungeon inspector (<em>Certified</em>), a necromancer's tea house (<em>The Long Steep</em>), and an apprentice cartographer in a world that rearranges nightly (<em>Redrawn</em>).</p>
<p><strong><code>samples/</code></strong> — the 3×3 matrix, one file per concept with the same scene in all three voices, so they can be compared blind. Each concept stress-tests something different: bureaucratic comedy, grief handled at close range, and wonder/worldbuilding respectively. My own read after writing them: Voice C is the strongest fit for high-slice-of-life LitRPG and the most Duane-approvable, but it carries the biggest pace-sag risk at daily volume; Voice B is the most sustainable at industrial cadence; Voice A is the most distinctive but the hardest to keep from tipping into pastiche. A blend anchored in B's discipline with C's sensory warmth is where I'd probe next — but read the samples first and tell me what lands.</p>
<p>Sources: RR forum threads on <a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/133747">chapter length</a>, <a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/103370">optimal length</a>, <a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/114828">stat-screen tolerance</a>, <a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/130367">statbox necessity</a>, <a href="https://www.royalroad.com/forums/thread/139555">system-message formatting</a>, and Beware of Chicken analyses at <a href="https://fanfiaddict.com/review-beware-of-chicken-a-xianxia-cultivation-novel-beware-of-chicken-1-by-casualfarmer/">FanFiAddict</a> and <a href="https://immortalcultivationhub.com/beware-of-chicken-cultivation-novel/">Immortal Cultivation Hub</a>.</p>
</div>


<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>I think of these voices I like the practitioner the best, but I think the tone is a bit off. All of these end up a little maudlin for my tastes to be honest, I think I'd like a bit more wry humour in it. Pull it all a bit more in the direction of pratchett. The story we're going to be aiming to tell is definitely aiming to be funny. Some of that can be through the voice of the characters showing through in the narration.</p>
<p>I think the samples don't quite work for me.</p>
<p>BTW one of my favourite pieces of royal road fiction is https://www.royalroad.com/fiction/35125/avaunt</p>
<p>It's not the story we'll be writing exactly, but I think it can be mined heavily for influence.</p></blockquote>

</div>

<details><summary>contents of notes/avaunt-influence.md</summary>
<h1>Avaunt — influence notes</h1>
<p>Source: <a href="https://www.royalroad.com/fiction/35125/avaunt">Avaunt</a> by AMBLE.<br />
Read: prologue, chapter one. Tags: Comedy, Female Lead, Action, Adventure,<br />
High Fantasy. Not our story, but a rich vein to mine.</p>
<h2>What the prose is actually doing</h2>
<p><strong>1. A narrator wider than any skull, with opinions.</strong> The camera moves<br />
freely and editorializes constantly. The signature construction is the<br />
<em>self-image vs. reality</em> pivot:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>In his own mind, Galar Kayle had taught his son an important lesson about<br />
appreciating the fortunate hand life had dealt him; in reality, he had<br />
taught his son not to get caught, which is a far more common lesson for<br />
children to take to heart.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>That last clause — generalizing outward from the character to humanity —<br />
is pure Pratchett machinery. Note it's a <em>kind</em> cruelty: the narrator is<br />
ruthless about what people actually are, but never contemptuous of them<br />
for being it.</p>
<p><strong>2. Unlovely, specific physical description.</strong> Cheis is introduced by a<br />
long, loving inventory of an aggressively plain face; Varbo is &quot;an<br />
ambulatory beard attached to a large keg.&quot; The comedy of description comes<br />
from precision, not from mockery — and the plainness is itself a statement<br />
(a renowned hero who is fat, plain, snaggle-toothed, and completely<br />
unbothered).</p>
<p><strong>3. Comic escalation resolved by competence.</strong> The tavern-menace scene runs<br />
a standard bullying escalation, then detonates it with a name drop and<br />
lets the aftermath be funny (two gang members &quot;later agreed to be the wisest<br />
of their number, simply departed&quot;). The heroine's power is expressed through<br />
everyone else's reaction and her own total unconcern — she orders stew and<br />
sleeps in the stables.</p>
<p><strong>4. Teeth.</strong> The funny prologue ends: &quot;By the next sunset, every man,<br />
woman, and child in Morhelm would be dead.&quot; The comedy is a surface tension<br />
over real stakes and real darkness, and the narrator will state horror as<br />
flatly as it states jokes. This is also very Pratchett (and very Bujold):<br />
the humour is not a safety guarantee.</p>
<p><strong>5. Jokes carried by syntax, not by gags.</strong> &quot;Tea was produced, denounced,<br />
and then consumed with ill grace.&quot; &quot;A mood so foul that it was in danger of<br />
qualifying as a political movement.&quot; &quot;Tebes was good at communication.&quot;<br />
The unit of humour is the sentence-shape: zeugma, lists with a swerve,<br />
deadpan understatement after implied violence.</p>
<p><strong>6. Character-flavoured narration.</strong> When the camera is near Linduin, the<br />
narration inherits his adolescent self-dramatization and then gently<br />
punctures it; near the burgon, it goes coarse and shrewd. The narrator<br />
modulates per scene without losing its own identity — this is the &quot;character<br />
voice showing through the narration&quot; effect we want.</p>
<h2>What to take</h2>
<ul>
<li>The self-image/reality pivot and the generalizing final clause.</li>
<li>Description as inventory-with-a-swerve; plain people rendered lovingly.</li>
<li>Competence as the resolution of comic escalation.</li>
<li>Permission for darkness stated flatly; comedy as tension, not padding.</li>
<li>Sentence-level wit as the default register, all the time, not deployed<br />
in designated &quot;funny bits.&quot;</li>
</ul>
<h2>What to leave</h2>
<ul>
<li>The prose occasionally over-eggs (three adjectives where one would do,<br />
&quot;palpably,&quot; &quot;the ugly miasma of derision&quot;). We hold a tighter line.</li>
<li>Very long unbroken paragraphs of backstory summary early in chapter one —<br />
charming here, but risky at daily cadence; we'd break these up with scene.</li>
</ul>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of notes/voices.md</summary>
<p><em>old:</em></p>
<hr />
<h2>Blind-test tells</h2>
<p><em>new:</em></p>
<hr />
<h2>Voice D — &quot;The Wry Practitioner&quot; (current working voice)</h2>
<p>Feedback on round one: the Practitioner won on warmth and texture but ran<br />
maudlin — every sample resolved into a lump-in-the-throat beat, and the<br />
humour was too gentle to carry a story that is <em>aiming to be funny</em>. The fix<br />
is a fusion, pulled toward Pratchett and calibrated against<br />
<a href="https://www.royalroad.com/fiction/35125/avaunt">Avaunt</a><br />
(see avaunt-influence.md): keep C's sensory attention and love of craft,<br />
replace its reverence with A's narrator-with-opinions, and make wit the<br />
default register of every paragraph rather than a seasoning.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Distance:</strong> close-ish third, but the narrator is wider than the POV's<br />
skull and knows it — free to note what a character believes about<br />
themselves and then report the less flattering truth, and to generalize<br />
outward from a character to people in general in a closing clause.</li>
<li><strong>The narration inherits the scene's character.</strong> Near a fussy character<br />
the prose fusses; near a laconic one it clips. Character voice shows<br />
through the narration itself, not just in dialogue — but the narrator's<br />
own identity (dry, precise, fond) never dissolves.</li>
<li><strong>Rhythm:</strong> the sentence is the unit of wit. Zeugma, lists that swerve on<br />
the last item, deadpan understatement after implied violence, the<br />
precise-wrong word. Long build, short drop. No designated funny bits: the<br />
default register is wry and the jokes are continuous but load-bearing.</li>
<li><strong>Warmth without syrup:</strong> the craft-and-food-and-weather attention stays,<br />
but rendered with the affectionate ruthlessness of someone who has<br />
actually done the work — the sourdough is described by the ways it can go<br />
wrong. People are liked <em>and</em> seen clearly; fondness is expressed through<br />
accuracy, never through the prose getting misty.</li>
<li><strong>At emotional peaks:</strong> the irony steps back but does not leave the room.<br />
One plain sentence is allowed to land; then a character or the world<br />
undercuts the moment before it can start congratulating itself. Sentiment<br />
is <em>earned by being interrupted.</em> If a scene ends on a swell of feeling,<br />
the ending is wrong — end one beat after, on the joke or the chore.</li>
<li><strong>Teeth:</strong> the comedy is not a safety guarantee. Darkness gets stated<br />
flatly when it comes, and the humour reads as how these people survive<br />
their world, not as evidence that nothing bad can happen.</li>
<li><strong>Risks to police:</strong> narrator smugness (asides that sneer rather than<br />
observe); joke-density crowding pace; the Pratchett pastiche failure mode<br />
(Capitalized Abstractions, footnote whimsy, &quot;million-to-one&quot; cadences —<br />
banned as constructions, not just as phrases).</li>
</ul>
<hr />
<h2>Blind-test tells</h2>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of notes/concepts-round2.md</summary>
<h1>Round-two concepts (voice calibration)</h1>
<p>Round one's concepts leaned too whimsical-melancholy — anxious dungeons and<br />
grieving widows invite exactly the maudlin register we're steering away from.<br />
These three are built to be funny <em>at premise level</em>: adult protagonists,<br />
comedy of competence, institutions behaving sincerely in absurd circumstances,<br />
and room for teeth. Still disposable; the real concept comes from DRMacIver.</p>
<h2>Concept 1 — <em>Room Sixteen</em></h2>
<p>Gerda Voss, fifty-one, spent thirty years cooking for a mercenary company and<br />
retired the day she inherited her sister's coaching inn — which turns out to<br />
sit directly on the Heroes' Road, four days' walk from the Splintered Vale<br />
dungeon complex. Adventurers are her clientele: young, loud, insured against<br />
death but not against stupidity, and convinced the innkeeper is an NPC. The<br />
System, which has never known what to do with support staff, grants her the<br />
[Innkeeper] class and starts issuing quests like &quot;Prevent the party in Room<br />
Sixteen from summoning anything before checkout.&quot; Progression through<br />
hospitality, logistics, and the slow revelation that innkeepers on the Heroes'<br />
Road are the actual load-bearing infrastructure of heroism, and always have<br />
been.</p>
<p><strong>Sample scene:</strong> a five-person adventuring party checks in, and Gerda<br />
enforces the house rules.</p>
<h2>Concept 2 — <em>Requisitioned</em></h2>
<p>For twenty-two years Odile Grell ran procurement for the Dark Lord Vasch —<br />
feeding forty thousand orcs on a blighted-land budget is the hardest logistics<br />
job in the world, and she was magnificent at it. Then a hero did the<br />
traditional thing, the tower fell, and the victorious kingdoms issued a<br />
general amnesty for &quot;non-combatant dark-aligned personnel.&quot; Now she's opened a<br />
general store in a small border town, where her System sheet still reads<br />
[Quartermaster of the Black Host — Evil, Administrative] and refuses all<br />
appeals to reclassify. Slice of life of a woman applying siege-grade<br />
competence to village commerce, while ex-minions drift into town looking for<br />
work, the locals slowly discover that Evil ran a much better supply chain than<br />
Good ever did, and the question of where forty thousand demobilized orcs went<br />
quietly refuses to stay off-page.</p>
<p><strong>Sample scene:</strong> market day; Odile negotiates with the town's incumbent<br />
merchant cartel, who have decided to run her out of business.</p>
<h2>Concept 3 — <em>Out Calls</em></h2>
<p>Whin Harrower is the only licensed monster veterinarian for three valleys —<br />
[Beast Chirurgeon], a class the System files under Combat because it involves<br />
touching monsters, a categorization Whin has appealed eleven times. The<br />
practice is pure James Herriot with scales: cockatrice mites, a gravid dire<br />
sow, a farm dog that got into the alchemical runoff and now briefly<br />
prophesies. Farmers pay late, in produce, or in dungeon shares of dubious<br />
legality; every level-up assumes she killed the patient rather than cured it.<br />
Progression through veterinary practice in a world whose ecology runs on<br />
dungeon overflow — and whose livestock is getting stranger every season, which<br />
somebody ought to be writing down.</p>
<p><strong>Sample scene:</strong> Whin is called out at dawn to a farm where something is<br />
wrong with the cockatrices, accompanied by a new apprentice with a combat<br />
class and opinions.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of samples/round2/room-sixteen.md</summary>
<h1><em>Room Sixteen</em> — sample scene, Voice D (Wry Practitioner)</h1>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> a five-person adventuring party checks in, and Gerda enforces the<br />
house rules.</p>
<hr />
<p>The party arrived an hour before sunset, which Gerda had come to recognize as<br />
the traditional time: late enough that they'd earned their exhaustion, early<br />
enough to pretend they'd planned it.</p>
<p>There were five of them, which there always were. She'd asked a wizard about<br />
it once, and he'd explained at length about action economies and the sacred<br />
geometry of the pentad, and she'd concluded — watching him fail to split a<br />
bill five ways — that adventurers travelled in fives for the same reason<br />
sheep moved in flocks, and that in both cases the arrangement was doing more<br />
thinking than any individual member.</p>
<p>The one in front had the good armor and the better jaw, and he leaned on her<br />
counter with the easy confidence of a young man who had never once been<br />
charged for damages.</p>
<p>&quot;Innkeeper! Five beds, stabling for six, and your finest—&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Three rooms, the stables are full, and the finest thing in this building is<br />
the porridge, which you'll have in the morning and thank me.&quot; She opened the<br />
ledger. &quot;Names.&quot;</p>
<p>He blinked, recovered, and produced a smile she assumed had opened a number<br />
of doors and at least one tomb. &quot;We are the Dawnbreakers. Perhaps you've<br />
heard of—&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Names people gave you.&quot;</p>
<p>The names, extracted, were Piet, Sallow, Herrick, Vess, and Mad Agnes, of<br />
whom Mad Agnes looked to be the sanest by a comfortable margin, being the<br />
only one who'd started unloading the mule without being told. Gerda wrote<br />
them in her slow, square hand while the System hung its quiet blue lamp at<br />
the edge of her sight:</p>
<p><em>Quest issued: The Dawnbreakers (Party, Bronze-rank, Insured). Objective:<br />
all five alive at checkout. Bonus objective: all sixteen windows.</em></p>
<p>The System had opinions about her windows. Gerda had opinions about the<br />
System — chiefly that something that could resurrect a swordsman from a<br />
smear ought to be able to make him wipe his boots, and declined to — but<br />
they had, over two years, arrived at a working relationship, the way you do<br />
with any large institution: low expectations, kept meticulously.</p>
<p>&quot;House rules,&quot; she said, and put the slate on the counter, and watched five<br />
faces do what faces always did at the slate. &quot;Weapons peace-bonded past the<br />
taproom door. No open flame in the rooms — that includes you, dear, don't<br />
look wounded, I can smell the brimstone from here. Nothing summoned on the<br />
premises. Nothing <em>ritually</em> summoned within a hundred yards of the<br />
premises. If it eats hay, it sleeps in the yard; if it eats souls, it sleeps<br />
past the property line. Breakfast is at six.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Six,&quot; said the jaw, in the voice of a man encountering the concept.</p>
<p>&quot;The dungeon opens at eight,&quot; Gerda said. &quot;It's four days off. You'll want<br />
an early start, and you'll want it fed, because the last party that skipped<br />
my porridge is a rockslide now.&quot; This was, strictly speaking, unfair — the<br />
Grey Company had died of overconfidence, not of hunger, though in her<br />
experience the two shared a pantry. But a good innkeeper keeps certain<br />
stories the way she keeps a fire banked: not for warmth, exactly. For use.</p>
<p>Vess — the small one at the back, robes, ink on both hands, the kind that<br />
read the whole slate twice — raised a hand. Nobody had raised a hand at<br />
Gerda since the mercenary company, and she felt a small, unprofessional<br />
warmth toward the girl at once.</p>
<p>&quot;It says no experiments in the rooms,&quot; said Vess. &quot;Define 'experiment'?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Anything you'd do twice to see if the first time was a fluke.&quot;</p>
<p>Vess considered this with the expression of a scholar finding the definition<br />
regrettably watertight, and lowered her hand.</p>
<p>They signed. They always signed — the young ones because rules were scenery<br />
to them, like weather, like innkeepers; the smart ones because they'd<br />
already noticed that every party that stayed here came back down the Road<br />
alive, and had begun, quietly, to wonder about the porridge. Gerda handed<br />
over three keys and did not smile until she was through the kitchen door,<br />
because a professional does her gloating in private.</p>
<p>Then she got the big pot down. Six eaters, one mule, sixteen windows, and<br />
whatever was making the scratching sound in Piet's pack that nobody had<br />
mentioned. It was going to be one of those nights, and porridge doesn't<br />
soak itself.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of samples/round2/requisitioned.md</summary>
<h1><em>Requisitioned</em> — sample scene, Voice D (Wry Practitioner)</h1>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> market day; Odile negotiates with the town's incumbent merchant<br />
cartel, who have decided to run her out of business.</p>
<hr />
<p>The Merchants' Association of Little Hobb came to see Odile on market day,<br />
all four of them, in their good coats. They came, as such delegations do, to<br />
be reasonable — which is a thing people are mostly at you, like weather.</p>
<p>&quot;Mistress Grell.&quot; Aldous Pike, chandler, chairman, and owner of the second-<br />
best scales in town, removed his hat. In Pike's own estimation this made him<br />
disarming. &quot;We thought it neighborly to call. There's been talk about your<br />
prices.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;There has,&quot; Odile agreed. &quot;I set them.&quot;</p>
<p>There'd been talk since the day she'd opened, most of it conducted at a<br />
volume intended to be overheard: about her prices, which were fair; about<br />
her stock, which was reliable; and about her previous employer, whose name<br />
the town handled the way you'd handle a wasp in a jar — frequently, with<br />
fascination, and never quite admitting the fascination. Her System sheet was<br />
public record, as the amnesty required. [Quartermaster of the Black Host —<br />
Evil, Administrative]. She'd appealed the classification twice since<br />
arriving. The System had responded, both times, in its serene blue<br />
copperplate: <em>Classification reflects service history. Evil (Administrative)<br />
carries no penalty to Commerce skills. Have a productive day.</em></p>
<p>It did not carry a penalty to Commerce skills. It carried Pike, hat in hand,<br />
with three grocers behind him arranged in what a military eye couldn't help<br />
noting was a wedge.</p>
<p>&quot;The Association has standards,&quot; Pike said. &quot;Agreed margins. Keeps things<br />
orderly. Undercut the margin and it's chaos — no offense meant — and we<br />
thought, given your situation, you'd want to be seen cooperating.&quot;</p>
<p>He said <em>your situation</em> the way he'd rehearsed it, with a little pause on<br />
either side, like a man setting down something heavy and valuable. It was<br />
the pause that did it. Odile had been threatened by professionals — she had<br />
once stood in front of Vasch the Undying while he peeled a subordinate out<br />
of existence for rounding an inventory to the nearest gross, and had<br />
handed him the corrected figures while the smoke settled — and she found<br />
that being menaced by a chandler produced mostly a kind of nostalgia.</p>
<p>&quot;Sit down, Master Pike,&quot; she said. &quot;I'll make tea.&quot;</p>
<p>This alarmed them more than anything she could have shouted, which she knew,<br />
which is why she did it. People who have decided you are a monster arrive<br />
braced for the monster; hospitality gets in under the shield rim.</p>
<p>She made the tea properly and set out the shortbread, and while they held<br />
their cups like hostages she took down her ledger and opened it facing them,<br />
because twenty-two years of requisition hearings had taught her that the<br />
truly frightening thing in any room is an accurate document.</p>
<p>&quot;Your agreed margin is eleven percent,&quot; she said. &quot;You take fourteen and<br />
call it eleven, because Wetherell's scales are short and everyone weighs at<br />
Wetherell's.&quot; Wetherell, third grocer from the left, made a noise like a<br />
kettle deciding against it. &quot;I take nine, honestly, and I still profit,<br />
because my wagons come in full and yours come back from Marsh Fair a third<br />
empty. You're not losing to my prices, gentlemen. You're losing to your<br />
carters, who steal, because you pay them to steal — sixty a season and<br />
first pick of 'spoilage'? You'd do better hiring actual thieves. Thieves<br />
have professional pride.&quot;</p>
<p>Silence, of the specific market-day kind, with a pig complaining somewhere<br />
outside and Pike's tea getting cold in front of him.</p>
<p>&quot;I'll not be lectured on honest business,&quot; Pike managed, &quot;by the Black<br />
Host's—&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Quartermaster. Say it, it's on the sign.&quot; She turned the ledger back<br />
around. &quot;The Host marched four hundred leagues on the stores I moved, and<br />
no one stole from my wagons twice. I fed forty thousand on mud and spite,<br />
Master Pike, and I never once shorted a scale, because my employer had<br />
standards about numbers.&quot; She let that sit, precisely as long as it needed<br />
to. &quot;You may keep your margin. I'll keep mine. Or the Association can hire<br />
me to fix your carting contracts — my consulting rate is on the wall — and<br />
we'll all profit, which I'd prefer. Evil was never against profit. Profit<br />
was the department I ran.&quot;</p>
<p>They left in disorder, which is to say in a wedge pointing the other way,<br />
and Wetherell, last through the door, hesitated — visibly, achingly — in<br />
front of the consulting rate before Pike's voice pulled him out into the<br />
market noise.</p>
<p>Odile cleared the cups and allowed herself one small satisfaction, which was<br />
professional: nobody had touched the shortbread, and shortbread kept.</p>
<p><em>Commerce has reached Level 14,</em> the System observed. <em>Evil (Administrative)<br />
remains without penalty. Have a productive day.</em></p>
<p>&quot;Working on it,&quot; said Odile.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of samples/round2/out-calls.md</summary>
<h1><em>Out Calls</em> — sample scene, Voice D (Wry Practitioner)</h1>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> Whin is called out at dawn to a farm where something is wrong with<br />
the cockatrices, accompanied by a new apprentice with a combat class and<br />
opinions.</p>
<hr />
<p>The message arrived before dawn, as messages about cockatrices generally do,<br />
cockatrices being at their worst in the hour when nobody wants to deal with<br />
them. It had been dictated to the miller's boy, who had run four miles to<br />
deliver it and got it down to five words: &quot;Harrower. Birds is wrong. Skene's.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Wrong how?&quot; asked Tam, in the cart, as the light came up grey over the fell.</p>
<p>&quot;That's the interesting question, isn't it.&quot; Whin took the ford at the angle<br />
that didn't soak the instrument box, a piece of local knowledge that had cost<br />
her two instrument boxes. &quot;With Skene's flock it could be anything from mites<br />
to a curse. He grazes them on the old barrow field, which I've told him about,<br />
and he feeds them scraps from the rendering shed, which I've also told him<br />
about, and Skene holds advice the way a sieve holds soup.&quot;</p>
<p>Tam nodded and checked his sword, which was his answer to most veterinary<br />
questions so far. He'd come down from the academy three weeks ago with<br />
[Beast Warden] at Level 11, a combat class, a good seat on a horse, and the<br />
settled conviction — the academy's parting gift to all its graduates — that<br />
the countryside was a dungeon with scenery. He was not stupid. He was<br />
educated, which takes longer to fix.</p>
<p>Skene's yard, when they rattled into it, contained Skene, hopping; Skene's<br />
wife, stationary, with the folded arms of a woman who had said her piece in<br />
about 1140 and seen no cause to amend it; and the stone statue of a fox.</p>
<p>The fox was mid-leap. Even in granite you could see it had been having the<br />
best moment of its life right up until the end, which, to be fair to the<br />
cockatrice, foxes give.</p>
<p>&quot;Well, the birds can't be too wrong,&quot; said Whin, getting down. &quot;Somebody's<br />
eyes are in working order.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;That's the trouble,&quot; said Skene. &quot;That's Petra's work, that fox, and it's<br />
the only stoning this month. Rest of them's gone soft-eyed. Look at 'em.&quot;</p>
<p>Whin looked. The run held thirty cockatrices, and a cockatrice run at dawn<br />
should be a war crime in feathers — shrieking, sparring, the young cocks<br />
glaring holes in the fence posts for practice. This lot drooped on their<br />
perches like a convocation of wet umbrellas. Combs grey at the edges. Third<br />
eyelids showing, milk-white, the gaze that should curdle a pond gone as<br />
dangerous as porridge.</p>
<p>&quot;Right,&quot; she said, and opened the box. &quot;Tam. What kills you first in that<br />
run?&quot;</p>
<p>Tam surveyed it with parade-ground crispness. &quot;The gaze, ma'am.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Petra could stone you, aye. The other twenty-nine, today, couldn't stiffen<br />
cream.&quot; She handed him the smoked-glass goggles anyway, because dead<br />
apprentices reflect poorly on a practice. &quot;So what kills you first?&quot;</p>
<p>He was silent long enough for it to count as thinking. &quot;...The beaks?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;The beaks. Sick bird's still a bird. Gaze is glamour; beak is sincere.&quot;<br />
She gloved up. &quot;You'll learn more from poultry than from anything with a<br />
bounty on it. Poultry doesn't monologue.&quot;</p>
<p>The nearest hen barely fought the examination, which told Whin more than the<br />
comb did. Wattle cold. Crop half-full since yesterday. Breath sweet — not<br />
rot-sweet, stone-sweet, like a quarry after rain. She'd read about that<br />
smell in Marrow's <em>Petrifying Fowl</em>, in the chapter everyone skips because<br />
it never comes up, and felt the small guilty joy of a professional whose<br />
morning has just become a journal article.</p>
<p><em>Diagnosis check passed,</em> the System announced. <em>Combat experience awarded:<br />
0. Threat neutralized: pending.</em></p>
<p>The System filed her under Combat and marked every cure as an inconclusive<br />
battle. Eleven appeals. It had answered the last one with what she could<br />
only interpret as sarcasm: <em>Recommendation: engage more threats.</em></p>
<p>&quot;It's in the gaze-gland,&quot; she said, straightening. &quot;They're not sick, Tam,<br />
they're <em>spent</em>. Something on this farm has been drinking the petrification<br />
out of thirty birds, nightly, and it left Petra for last because Petra's the<br />
strongest.&quot; She looked across the yard, past the fence, to the barrow field,<br />
where the grass grew in rings and she had told Skene, and told him, and told<br />
him. &quot;And it'll be back tonight for her.&quot;</p>
<p>Tam's hand went to the sword. Skene's wife unfolded her arms — a mobilization.</p>
<p>&quot;Ma'am. <em>Now</em> is it a combat class problem?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;It's a stay-up-all-night-in-a-cold-barn problem,&quot; said Whin, which in her<br />
experience was the true name of most combat class problems. &quot;Fetch the<br />
lanterns. And Skene — tea, and I'll want to see that rendering shed, and if<br />
there's a barrow open in that field, you're digging the fence line <em>today</em>,<br />
because I bill double for anything with a memory.&quot;</p>
</details>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Round two is done and committed. What changed:</p>
<p><strong>Avaunt study</strong> (<code>notes/avaunt-influence.md</code>) — I pulled the prologue and chapter one directly and analyzed the prose. The load-bearing techniques: the self-image-vs-reality pivot with a generalizing final clause (&quot;…which is a far more common lesson for children to take to heart&quot;); unlovely-but-loving physical description; comic escalation resolved by competence; sentence-shape wit as the constant register (&quot;Tea was produced, denounced, and then consumed with ill grace&quot;); and real teeth — the funny prologue ends with the whole village dead. Also noted what <em>not</em> to take (occasional over-egging, long backstory paragraphs). And yes please to the epub — two chapters shows me the voice but not how it sustains, paces arcs, or balances comedy against darkness over volume. Put it anywhere in the repo or scratchpad and point me at it.</p>
<p><strong>Revised voice</strong> (<code>voices.md</code>, &quot;Voice D — The Wry Practitioner&quot;) — Practitioner's sensory warmth and craft-love, fused with a narrator who is wider than the POV's skull and has opinions, with wit as the default register rather than seasoning. The anti-maudlin rule is structural: sentiment is earned by being interrupted, and if a scene ends on a swell of feeling the ending is wrong — end one beat later, on the joke or the chore. Also an explicit ban on the Pratchett-pastiche failure modes (Capitalized Abstractions, footnote whimsy) since we want his approval, not his costume.</p>
<p><strong>New concepts</strong> (<code>notes/concepts-round2.md</code>) — the round-one set invited melancholy at premise level (anxious dungeon, grieving widow), so these are built funny from the ground up, with adult competence at the center:</p>
<ol>
<li><em><strong>Room Sixteen</strong></em> — a mercenary company's retired cook inherits an inn on the Heroes' Road; adventurers think she's an NPC; innkeepers are heroism's actual infrastructure.</li>
<li><em><strong>Requisitioned</strong></em> — the Dark Lord's procurement officer, amnestied after the tower fell, opens a village shop; the System refuses to reclassify her off [Evil, Administrative].</li>
<li><em><strong>Out Calls</strong></em> — the only monster veterinarian for three valleys; James Herriot with cockatrices; the System files her under Combat and won't hear appeals.</li>
</ol>
<p><strong>Samples</strong> (<code>samples/round2/</code>, one scene each in the new voice; round one archived to <code>samples/round1/</code>). Each ends on a chore or a bill, not a feeling: Gerda's checkout quest and unsoaked porridge, Odile's untouched shortbread and consulting rate, Whin billing double for anything with a memory.</p>
<p>My own read: <em>Requisitioned</em> has the strongest comic engine (institutional absurdity + real teeth in the forty-thousand-orcs question), <em>Out Calls</em> is the most naturally slice-of-life sustainable at daily cadence, <em>Room Sixteen</em> is the most conventionally Royal-Road-shaped. Curious whether the tone landed where you wanted it — and whether any of these concepts are close enough to be worth a second scene.</p>
</div>
