<details><summary>contents of notes/llm-tells.md</summary>
<h1>LLM prose tells — research and self-audit</h1>
<p>The problem, as reported: the Wry Practitioner samples are good but read as<br />
LLM-generated in a way that's hard to point at. This note collects what<br />
readers and critics have actually identified, then audits our own round-two<br />
samples against it. The surface tells (vocabulary lists) are the least of it;<br />
the deep tells are rhythmic and structural.</p>
<h2>Surface tells (easy to police, least important)</h2>
<ul>
<li><strong>Vocabulary:</strong> delve, tapestry, testament, intricate, pivotal, underscore,<br />
nestled, vibrant, profound. Also copula-avoidance (&quot;serves as,&quot; &quot;stands<br />
as&quot;) and marketing verbs (&quot;boasts,&quot; &quot;features&quot;) where &quot;is&quot; and &quot;has&quot; would<br />
do. (<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Signs_of_AI_writing">Wikipedia's catalog</a>)</li>
<li><strong>Em-dash density.</strong> Not wrong, but statistically a flag, and our samples<br />
are saturated with them.</li>
<li><strong>Negative parallelism:</strong> &quot;not X, but Y&quot; / &quot;It's not X. It's Y.&quot; Cited<br />
everywhere as <em>the</em> construction (<a href="https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=45407571">HN discussion</a>,<br />
<a href="https://www.ignorance.ai/p/the-field-guide-to-ai-slop">Field Guide to AI Slop</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Triads,</strong> especially with a swerve on the third item. Humans use them;<br />
LLMs use them <em>constantly</em>.</li>
</ul>
<h2>Deep tells (the actual problem)</h2>
<ol>
<li><strong>The prose is never at rest.</strong> Every sentence is shaped; every paragraph<br />
lands a turn; there are no plain load-bearing sentences that just move a<br />
body across a room. Critics describe the effect as every line &quot;auditioning&quot;<br />
(<a href="https://medium.com/@tdoherty_96508/the-cutesy-cadence-crisis-why-ai-keeps-writing-like-a-bad-preacher-and-why-it-needs-to-stop-67636451846a">Medium, &quot;cutesy cadence&quot;</a>).<br />
Human wit gets its force from surrounding flatness. Uniform sparkle reads<br />
as machine because it is: the model optimizes each sentence locally and<br />
nothing tells it to spend less.</li>
<li><strong>Aphorism epidemic.</strong> Related but distinct: the habit of converting every<br />
observation into a portable epigram. One per scene is a style; six per<br />
scene is a tell. The epigrams are also <em>frictionless</em> — they generalize<br />
(&quot;which is a thing people are mostly at you, like weather&quot;) rather than<br />
staying stuck to the particular.</li>
<li><strong>Over-explained theme.</strong> <a href="https://aiweekly.co/alerts/storyscope-ai-fiction-over-explains-its-themes-77-of-the-time">StoryScope's analysis</a><br />
found AI narrators explicitly state the theme 77% of the time vs 52% for<br />
humans. The narrator keeps telling the reader what the story is about.</li>
<li><strong>Self-annotating cleverness.</strong> The narration performs a device and then<br />
explains it (&quot;…which she knew, which is why she did it&quot;). Human writers<br />
trust the reader; the model hedges its jokes by footnoting them.</li>
<li><strong>Tidy button endings.</strong> Every scene closes with a neat callback that<br />
clicks shut like a box. One critic's phrase: &quot;surface polish with nothing<br />
underneath.&quot; Real scenes are allowed to end slightly open, off-center, or<br />
on an unglamorous beat that doesn't rhyme with the opening.</li>
<li><strong>Uniform competence in dialogue.</strong> Everyone speaks in calibrated deadpan;<br />
every character is quick. No one fumbles, repeats themselves, trails off,<br />
or says something merely functional. The wit has no ecosystem.</li>
<li><strong>Predictable rhythm swings:</strong> long ornate build → short punchy fragment,<br />
over and over. The <em>pattern itself</em> becomes the monotony<br />
(<a href="https://recordcrash.substack.com/p/how-to-identify-ai-written-web-fiction">Record Crash guide</a>).</li>
<li><strong>Visual-only concreteness.</strong> Detail skews toward what things look like;<br />
weight, effort, temperature, smell, and the body's experience of work are<br />
thin. (Notably, this is one place our samples are okay — keep it that way.)</li>
</ol>
<h2>Self-audit of round two</h2>
<p>All three samples exhibit tells 1, 2, 4, 5, 7 heavily, 3 and 6 moderately.<br />
Concrete instances (from <em>Requisitioned</em> alone): &quot;reasonable — which is a<br />
thing people are mostly at you, like weather&quot; (epigram, generalizing);<br />
&quot;hospitality gets in under the shield rim&quot; (epigram); &quot;the truly frightening<br />
thing in any room is an accurate document&quot; (epigram); &quot;which she knew, which<br />
is why she did it&quot; (self-annotation); &quot;You're not losing to my prices…<br />
you're losing to your carters&quot; (negative parallelism); &quot;frequently, with<br />
fascination, and never quite admitting the fascination&quot; (triad-with-swerve);<br />
&quot;nobody had touched the shortbread, and shortbread kept&quot; (button ending);<br />
plus wall-to-wall em dashes. Every paragraph turns. The Avaunt influence made<br />
the wit continuous; continuous wit at uniform density is exactly the machine<br />
voice.</p>
<p>Note the diagnosis is NOT &quot;less wit.&quot; Avaunt's wit is near-continuous too.<br />
The difference: Avaunt's jokes arise from <em>what happens and who people are</em><br />
(Varbo escaping through the kitchen; two gang members &quot;later agreed to be the<br />
wisest of their number&quot;) and its sentences spend most of their time plainly<br />
reporting events. Our samples' jokes arise from the narrator turning<br />
observations into ornaments. Event-wit over ornament-wit.</p>
<h2>Countermeasures (now part of the voice spec)</h2>
<ul>
<li><strong>Ration the epigrams:</strong> at most one narrator aphorism per scene, and it<br />
must stay stuck to its particulars, not float free as quotable wisdom.</li>
<li><strong>Plain sentences are mandatory.</strong> Most sentences should only report. The<br />
joke earns its shape from the flatness around it. If every paragraph in a<br />
page has a turn, cut turns until some don't.</li>
<li><strong>Never annotate a device.</strong> Do the trick; don't explain the trick.</li>
<li><strong>Vary the joke mechanism per scene</strong> (event, character reaction, timing,<br />
understatement) instead of defaulting to narrator ornament.</li>
<li><strong>Ban-list as constructions:</strong> &quot;not X, but Y&quot; and its cousins; triad with<br />
third-item swerve (max one per scene); long-build/short-drop used more<br />
than twice per scene.</li>
<li><strong>Em dashes:</strong> budget of roughly two per thousand words. Restructure, use<br />
commas, parentheses, or full stops.</li>
<li><strong>Endings:</strong> no callback buttons by default. End scenes off-center — on<br />
the next piece of work, an unresolved noise, someone leaving badly.</li>
<li><strong>Dialogue ecosystem:</strong> give characters different speeds. Some people in<br />
every scene are not witty. Let functional dialogue be functional.</li>
<li><strong>Theme stays unstated.</strong> If a sentence says what the story is about,<br />
cut it. The reader gets to do that job.</li>
</ul>
</details>

</div>

<details><summary>contents of notes/llm-tells.md</summary>
<p><em>old:</em></p>
<h2>Countermeasures (now part of the voice spec)</h2>
<p><em>new:</em></p>
<h2>The hydrogen jukebox frame</h2>
<p>Two essays that name the deep mechanism better than the tell-lists do:<br />
<a href="https://www.tumblr.com/nostalgebraist/778041178124926976/hydrogen-jukeboxes">nostalgebraist's &quot;hydrogen jukeboxes&quot;</a><br />
and <a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/nostalgebraists-hydrogen-jukeboxes">Scott Alexander's response</a>.</p>
<p>Nostalgebraist: LLM writing runs on the <em>eyeball kick</em> — Ginsberg's term for<br />
jamming an abstraction against something concrete (&quot;hydrogen jukebox&quot;) — as a<br />
push-button literariness signal. Abstract/concrete conjunctions, relentless<br />
personification, the ghost/echo/whisper/hum lexicon. The tricks are real<br />
tricks; good writers use them <em>to do things</em>, in service of an intent, while<br />
the model uses them because they reliably cue &quot;this sounds literary,&quot;<br />
regardless of what the passage needs.</p>
<p>Scott's refinement: taste is largely the discipline of <em>not</em> using cheaply<br />
executable, innately appealing tricks, because overexposure turns them<br />
grating; poor taste is trick-density. High pressure to perform + limited<br />
capacity to serve global intent = maximal seasoning.</p>
<p>This lands squarely on our samples. Our version of the eyeball kick is<br />
upmarket — &quot;steam came off it like a soul behaving itself,&quot; &quot;patient as a<br />
name on the tip of a tongue,&quot; &quot;menaced by a chandler produced mostly a kind<br />
of nostalgia&quot; — but it is the same button: an abstract-concrete flourish<br />
deployed at fixed intervals because each one locally sounds like good<br />
writing. The wit is real, but it is <em>seasoning applied at uniform rate</em><br />
rather than effects chosen by a governing intent for the scene.</p>
<p>The test to apply, per nostalgebraist: for any flourish, can we say what it<br />
is <em>doing</em> — for this scene, this character, this moment? &quot;Sounding like the<br />
voice&quot; is not an answer; that's the jukebox coin-slot. If the answer is only<br />
that it's a good line, it goes. A corollary: the effects worth keeping will<br />
mostly be <em>unrepeatable</em> — grown from this scene's specific material — which<br />
is exactly why the model's habit-flourishes feel interchangeable across<br />
stories.</p>
<h2>Countermeasures (now part of the voice spec)</h2>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of notes/voices.md</summary>
<p><em>old:</em></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Risks to police:</strong> narrator smugness (asides that sneer rather than<br />
observe); joke-density crowding pace; the Pratchett pastiche failure mode<br />
(Capitalized Abstractions, footnote whimsy, &quot;million-to-one&quot; cadences —<br />
banned as constructions, not just as phrases).</li>
</ul>
<p><em>new:</em></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Risks to police:</strong> narrator smugness (asides that sneer rather than<br />
observe); joke-density crowding pace; the Pratchett pastiche failure mode<br />
(Capitalized Abstractions, footnote whimsy, &quot;million-to-one&quot; cadences —<br />
banned as constructions, not just as phrases).</li>
</ul>
<h3>Revision (round three): de-LLMing the voice</h3>
<p>Round-two feedback: good, but recognizably LLM-flavoured. Diagnosis and full<br />
countermeasures in llm-tells.md; the binding rules are now part of this<br />
voice:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Prose at rest.</strong> Most sentences plainly report. A page where every<br />
paragraph turns is a page with too many turns. The wit's force comes from<br />
the flatness around it.</li>
<li><strong>Event-wit over ornament-wit.</strong> Jokes arise from what happens and who<br />
people are, not from the narrator decorating observations. (Avaunt's wit<br />
is continuous but mostly <em>event</em>-wit; that's the licence and the limit.)</li>
<li><strong>One narrator aphorism per scene, maximum,</strong> and it stays stuck to its<br />
particulars — no free-floating quotable wisdom.</li>
<li><strong>The jukebox test:</strong> every flourish must be doing something nameable for<br />
this scene, character, or moment. &quot;It's a good line&quot; is disqualifying —<br />
that's the coin-slot. Prefer effects that couldn't be transplanted to<br />
another story.</li>
<li><strong>Banned as habits:</strong> negative parallelism (&quot;not X, but Y&quot;); triad with<br />
third-item swerve (max one/scene); long-build-short-drop more than<br />
twice/scene; self-annotated devices (do the trick, never explain it);<br />
abstract-concrete simile as default decoration; the<br />
ghost/echo/whisper/hum lexicon; personification of objects as filler.</li>
<li><strong>Em-dash budget</strong> ~2 per 1,000 words.</li>
<li><strong>Endings off-center.</strong> No callback buttons that click shut. End on the<br />
next piece of work, an unresolved noise, a departure handled badly.</li>
<li><strong>Dialogue ecosystem:</strong> characters have different speeds; some people are<br />
not witty; functional lines stay functional; nobody fences unless fencing<br />
is characterization.</li>
<li><strong>Theme unstated.</strong> If a sentence says what the story is about, cut it.</li>
</ol>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of samples/round3/requisitioned-v2.md</summary>
<h1><em>Requisitioned</em> — sample scene, Voice D round three</h1>
<p>Same scene as round two's version, rewritten under the de-LLMing rules<br />
(voices.md, llm-tells.md): prose at rest, event-wit, one aphorism, no<br />
buttons. Kept for comparison; the old version stays in samples/round2/.</p>
<hr />
<p>The Merchants' Association of Little Hobb called on Odile on market day.<br />
There were four of them and they had dressed for the occasion, which meant<br />
that Aldous Pike was wearing his hat, and meant that he could remove it in<br />
the doorway.</p>
<p>&quot;Mistress Grell.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Master Pike.&quot; She finished weighing out Widow Annet's flour, tied the sack,<br />
counted the change into Annet's hand, and wished her a good morning. Annet<br />
left at about a third of her usual speed, and lingered outside the window,<br />
inspecting some onions she had already bought.</p>
<p>Pike put his hat on the counter. The three grocers behind him spread out<br />
among the shelves in a way that was presumably meant to be casual. One of<br />
them, Wetherell, picked up a tin of boot-grease and read the label with<br />
great attention, and went on reading it for the rest of the visit.</p>
<p>&quot;There's been talk,&quot; said Pike, &quot;about your prices.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Mm,&quot; said Odile.</p>
<p>&quot;The Association has agreed margins. Eleven percent. It keeps things<br />
orderly, stops any race to the bottom. All the established traders<br />
subscribe.&quot; He paused. &quot;We thought that a woman in your situation would want<br />
to be seen cooperating.&quot;</p>
<p>He gave <em>your situation</em> a little room on either side, so it could be<br />
admired. Odile had been threatened by better. She had spent twenty-two<br />
years in weekly requisition meetings with Vasch the Undying, a being who<br />
had once unmade a clerk over a rounding error while she stood close enough<br />
to smell the ozone, and who had then apologized to her, because the figures<br />
had been correct and interrupting a report was rude. It was not that Pike<br />
failed to frighten her. It was that the instrument was miscalibrated and<br />
she could hear it.</p>
<p>&quot;Sit down,&quot; she said. &quot;I'm making tea anyway.&quot;</p>
<p>Pike had prepared for several kinds of resistance, and sat down because tea<br />
was not among them. The two grocers who were not reading about boot-grease<br />
looked at each other and remained standing, at a loss for what to do that<br />
would be menacing near shortbread.</p>
<p>She made the tea and set out cups, and while it steeped she took the ledger<br />
from under the counter and opened it to the spring quarter, facing him.</p>
<p>&quot;Your eleven percent is fourteen,&quot; she said. &quot;Wetherell's scales run four<br />
drams light and everyone weighs at Wetherell's, so the margin's a fiction,<br />
but it's your fiction, and I don't care about it. Here is what I charge and<br />
here is what I pay. Look as long as you like.&quot;</p>
<p>Pike looked. He was slow at figures. She let him be slow, and drank her<br />
tea, and served a boy who came in for a pennyworth of nails while the<br />
silence went on. Wetherell had begun, very quietly, to weigh the tin of<br />
boot-grease in his hand.</p>
<p>&quot;You're buying your cartage from Marsh Fair,&quot; Pike said at last.</p>
<p>&quot;I'm buying it where you buy it. I'm just not being robbed. Your carters<br />
take sixty a season and first pick of what they can call spoiled, so on<br />
your wagons a great deal spoils. Mine doesn't. That's the whole of my<br />
advantage, Master Pike. You could have it too. I'd draw up the contracts<br />
for a fee, and the fee is on the wall there, under the string.&quot;</p>
<p>Three of them turned to read the card. Pike did not. He was looking at the<br />
open ledger, at her margins, with an expression she recognized from long<br />
experience of quartermasters caught short at an audit, which was the<br />
expression of a man rapidly revising the story he had planned to tell at<br />
the Association dinner.</p>
<p>&quot;The amnesty board,&quot; he said, &quot;meets quarterly. Renewals.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;It does. My compliance officer is Sergeant Hale, she takes my report on<br />
the first Monday of the month, and she buys her tobacco here.&quot; Odile<br />
turned the ledger back around. &quot;Anything else? Market day's my busy<br />
morning.&quot;</p>
<p>They went. Pike remembered his hat in the doorway and had to come back one<br />
step for it, which undid whatever the exit had been meant to establish.<br />
Through the window she watched them regroup by the well, Pike talking, the<br />
other two nodding, until Wetherell came trailing back in with his tin.</p>
<p>&quot;That's tuppence,&quot; said Odile.</p>
<p>He paid it and hesitated, tin in hand, half turned to the door. Widow Annet<br />
had come back in behind him for a second look at the shortbread, and he<br />
waited until she was arguing weights at the counter before he leaned in.</p>
<p>&quot;Four drams,&quot; he said. &quot;Who told you?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Nobody. I bought a pound of your raisins my first week and weighed it.&quot;</p>
<p>Wetherell took this in. &quot;Send us the carting thing anyway,&quot; he said, and<br />
left before she could name the fee twice.</p>
<p>The System spoke up while she was clearing the cups. <em>Commerce has reached<br />
Level 14. Classification: Evil (Administrative). No penalty applies.</em></p>
<p>Odile put the shortbread away, all but one piece, and got out the ink. If<br />
Wetherell wanted contracts he'd want them before the quarterly dinner, and<br />
the good copperplate took her longer than it used to.</p>
</details>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Done and committed. Here's what the research turned up and what I changed.</p>
<p><strong>The diagnosis</strong> (<code>notes/llm-tells.md</code>). The surface tells — em-dash density, &quot;not X, but Y&quot; constructions, triads with a swerve on the third item, the delve/tapestry lexicon — are documented everywhere and easy to police. The deeper problem, which is what you were sensing, is rhythmic and structural: the prose is never at rest (every sentence shaped, every paragraph landing a turn, no plain sentences doing plain work), an aphorism epidemic, self-annotated cleverness (&quot;…which she knew, which is why she did it&quot;), tidy callback endings that click shut, and every character speaking in the same calibrated deadpan. One study found AI narrators state their theme explicitly 77% of the time versus 52% for humans.</p>
<p>The two links you sent were the most useful things I read. Nostalgebraist's &quot;hydrogen jukebox&quot; essay names the mechanism exactly: LLMs push the abstract-concrete-conjunction button because it reliably cues &quot;literary,&quot; regardless of what the passage needs — and my samples were doing an upmarket version of precisely that (&quot;steam came off it like a soul behaving itself,&quot; &quot;patient as a name on the tip of a tongue&quot;). Scott's response reframes taste as the discipline of <em>not</em> using cheaply executable tricks at high density. I audited my own round-two samples against all of this in the notes file, with specific quoted offenses — <em>Requisitioned</em> alone had six free-floating epigrams. The key distinction I landed on: Avaunt's wit is near-continuous too, but it's <strong>event-wit</strong> (Varbo escaping through the kitchen) where mine was <strong>ornament-wit</strong> (the narrator decorating observations at a fixed rate).</p>
<p><strong>The voice spec</strong> (<code>voices.md</code>) now has nine binding round-three rules, the load-bearing ones being: most sentences just report; jokes come from events and character, not narrator decoration; one aphorism per scene maximum, stuck to its particulars; every flourish must pass the jukebox test (&quot;it's a good line&quot; is disqualifying); endings go off-center rather than clicking shut; and characters get different speeds, including unwitty ones.</p>
<p><strong>The demonstration</strong> (<code>samples/round3/requisitioned-v2.md</code>) — the merchant-cartel scene rewritten from scratch under the new rules, with round two's version kept for diffing. The jokes now live in events: Widow Annet lingering outside to eavesdrop, Wetherell spending the whole confrontation reading a boot-grease tin and then sneaking back in to ask &quot;who told you?&quot;, Pike having to come back one step for his hat. Vasch apologizes for interrupting a report because the figures were correct. It ends on ink and contracts, not a bow.</p>
<p>Worth a read side by side with the round-two version — I think the difference is audible. And the epub offer still stands if it does; a full read of Avaunt would help me calibrate how event-wit sustains over a few hundred thousand words.</p>
</div>

import re, html, glob, os
os.makedirs('text', exist_ok=True)
files = sorted(glob.glob('OEBPS/file*.xhtml'))
total = 0
for f in files:
    s = open(f, encoding='utf-8').read()
    title = re.search(r'&lt;title&gt;(.*?)&lt;/title&gt;', s)
    body = re.search(r'&lt;body[^&gt;]*&gt;(.*)&lt;/body&gt;', s, re.S).group(1)
    text = re.sub(r'&lt;/p&gt;', '\n\n', body)
    text = re.sub(r'&lt;[^&gt;]+&gt;', '', text)
    text = html.unescape(text)
    text = re.sub(r'\n{3,}', '\n\n', text).strip()
    n = len(text.split()); total += n
    out = f'text/{os.path.basename(f).replace(".xhtml",".txt")}'
    open(out, 'w').write((title.group(1) if title else '') + '\n\n' + text)
    print(os.path.basename(f), title.group(1) if title else '?', n)
print('TOTAL WORDS', total)
EOF</pre>
</details>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>All done and committed. What the full read of Avaunt yielded, beyond the two-chapter sample:</p>
<p><strong>The most important finding corrects our own notes.</strong> I'd claimed Avaunt's sentences &quot;spend most of their time plainly reporting&quot; — the readers found that's false in its comic threads, which run about one shaped joke per 2–3 sentences. What saves it from machine-uniformity is that rest is deployed at <em>scene scale</em> (whole POV threads and grief scenes go completely flat) and the jokes are built from plot, character biography, and system logic, so they're never the same trick twice. I've updated the voice rules accordingly: sentence-level restraint stays binding for us, but there's now an explicit scene-level silence tier, and chapter finals are allowed to click (cliffhanger/stinger/flash-forward) since that's the serial retention mechanic — with the flash-forward counting as the chapter's one aphorism.</p>
<p><strong>The biggest LitRPG lesson:</strong> Avaunt's system vocabulary isn't a world-overlay, it's <em>one character's cognitive idiom</em> — Cheis invented magic-as-software herself, and everyone else experiences magic as numinous mystery. That single choice generates the register-clash comedy, makes system talk characterize rather than gamify, and gives worldbuilding contrast for free. Strong candidate principle for our story: different POVs should experience the System differently. Related: the UI readout as the flattest possible camera (&quot;his power reserves had dipped to 3.19 ulasovs&quot; after leveling a city), and the rule that system logic must actually resolve plot or the jokes are stickers.</p>
<p><strong>Its instructive failures:</strong> jargon-stacking until fight choreography is illegible (the study proposes a hard rule — the physical event must be recoverable by a reader who skips every invented term), deflating <em>every</em> death until stakes numb, and rushing the father-son reunion its whole structure had spent chapters earning.</p>
<p>The study (<code>notes/avaunt-craft-study.md</code>, ~3,200 words) also has a full humor taxonomy with quotes, the four chapter-ending species, and eleven concrete additions cross-referenced against our existing rules. Ready for the real concept whenever you want to share it.</p>
</div>

<details><summary>contents of notes/premise.md</summary>
<h1>Premise (partial — full concept to come from DRMacIver)</h1>
<p>What we know so far:</p>
<ul>
<li>A fairly <strong>played-straight LitRPG plot</strong> with a classic Royal Road<br />
protagonist-figure: young(ish), progression-driven, well fleshed out,<br />
<em>not</em> a parody. Call this character the Hero for now.</li>
<li>The actual <strong>POV protagonist is someone else</strong>: much older, much more<br />
powerful, begrudgingly along for the ride and making the best of it.<br />
Reference points: Granny Weatherwax / Sam Vimes, but considerably more<br />
chill. Grumpy, somewhat cynical, fundamentally good, wants the best for<br />
everyone, willing to do the work.</li>
<li>The System will be <strong>classic LitRPG</strong>, not Avaunt's diegetic-idiom system.<br />
We are not copying the magic-as-software conceit.</li>
</ul>
<h2>Structural reading</h2>
<p>This is a <em>companion-narrator</em> structure — closer to Watson narrating Holmes<br />
than to a mentor subplot, except our Watson is the most dangerous person in<br />
the room. The genre plot runs at full sincerity in the Hero's storyline; the<br />
comedy, judgment, and theme live in the POV character's perception of it.<br />
That means we get to have it both ways, which is precisely the Royal Road<br />
sweet spot: readers who want the dings get real dings; readers who want the<br />
voice get the voice.</p>
<h2>Voice implications</h2>
<ol>
<li><strong>Event-wit is now structural.</strong> The gap between what the Hero's story<br />
thinks is happening (epic progression) and what the elder sees (a<br />
Tuesday, badly organized) generates comedy without the narrator<br />
decorating anything. This bakes our &quot;event-wit over ornament-wit&quot; rule<br />
into the premise itself — the single biggest de-LLMing win available.</li>
<li><strong>The System gets a double reading for free.</strong> Played straight for the<br />
Hero — real rules, real stakes, real progression the reader can invest<br />
in. Through the elder's eyes it's also an institution, and our POV<br />
character has decades of history with institutions. We can honor blue-box<br />
conventions sincerely (the Hero's sheet matters) while the POV's<br />
<em>reaction</em> to a box carries the wit. No parody needed; weariness is not<br />
mockery.</li>
<li><strong>Never mock the Hero.</strong> The Vimes/Weatherwax lineage is exacting here:<br />
both extend real respect to earnest people. The elder's cynicism aims at<br />
systems, gods, adventuring economics, and their own past — not at the<br />
Hero's sincerity. The Hero should regularly be <em>right</em> in ways that<br />
surprise the elder, or the structure curdles into 84k words of eye-rolling.<br />
The relationship arc (irritation → investment → something like love,<br />
never announced) is probably the story's real spine.</li>
<li><strong>Power without power-fantasy.</strong> A vastly strong POV kills tension unless<br />
the story's problems are the kind power doesn't solve: people, logistics,<br />
institutions, the Hero's growth, whatever the elder is along-for-the-ride<br />
<em>about</em>. Vimes's power never gets him out of anything; it gets him into<br />
things with more responsibility attached. Also implies restraint has a<br />
cost and reasons — the most interesting question about a powerful<br />
character is why they don't act.</li>
<li><strong>&quot;Chill&quot; is the calibration keyword.</strong> Weatherwax runs on suppressed<br />
fury, Vimes on barely-banked anger. Ours has <em>made peace</em> with more.<br />
That lowers the narration's temperature: less seething interiority, more<br />
dry patience; complaint as a comfortable habit rather than a wound. It<br />
also means when the temperature does rise, it registers hard — anger is<br />
a rare-event signal, budgeted like the aphorisms.</li>
<li><strong>Two registers, one voice.</strong> Avaunt's lesson about POV threads running<br />
at different wit densities applies <em>within</em> scenes here: Hero-material<br />
rendered with genre sincerity, elder-material rendered dry, and the<br />
narration (locked to the elder) modulating between them without a seam.<br />
The elder's competence-attention (our Practitioner inheritance) is the<br />
bridge: they take the Hero's problems seriously as <em>work</em> even while<br />
finding the framing ridiculous.</li>
<li><strong>Begrudging is a renewable engine.</strong> &quot;Along for the ride&quot; supplies each<br />
chapter's shape at daily cadence: the elder would rather not, does it<br />
anyway, does it well, denies caring. But the denial must erode over the<br />
long arc or it becomes a tic — plan the erosion.</li>
</ol>
<h2>Open questions for the full concept</h2>
<ul>
<li>Why is the elder along for the ride — what's the obligation, debt, or<br />
private motive? (This choice sets the whole story's emotional engine.)</li>
<li>Does the elder have a class/sheet, and do they engage with the System or<br />
decline to? (A high-level sheet glimpsed rarely is a great slow-burn<br />
mystery; RR readers love an obscured stat block far more than a shown one.)</li>
<li>Does the Hero know what the elder is?</li>
<li>Slice-of-life anchor: what's the domestic/craft ground the story returns<br />
to between plot beats?</li>
</ul>
</details>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Captured in <code>notes/premise.md</code> and committed. The short version of my read:</p>
<p><strong>Structurally, this is a companion-narrator story</strong> — Watson narrating Holmes, except Watson is the most dangerous person in the room. That lets the story have it both ways on Royal Road: the Hero's storyline delivers sincere progression and real dings for the genre audience, while the comedy and judgment live entirely in the elder's perception. Which means event-wit — the thing we identified as the anti-LLM-tell — is now baked into the premise: the gap between &quot;epic quest&quot; and &quot;a Tuesday, badly organized&quot; generates humor with no narrator decoration required.</p>
<p>The implications I flagged that most affect voice:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Never mock the Hero.</strong> The Weatherwax/Vimes lineage is exacting about this — their contempt goes to systems and gods, never to earnest people. The Hero needs to be regularly <em>right</em> in ways that surprise the elder, or the structure curdles into a long eye-roll. I suspect the irritation→investment arc is the story's actual spine.</li>
<li><strong>&quot;More chill&quot; lowers the narration's temperature.</strong> Weatherwax seethes; Vimes banks fury. Ours has made peace with more — so complaint reads as a comfortable habit, not a wound, and genuine anger becomes a rare-event signal worth budgeting like the aphorisms.</li>
<li><strong>Power needs problems it can't solve</strong> — people, logistics, the Hero's growth — and the elder's restraint should have visible reasons and costs. &quot;Why doesn't she just fix it&quot; is the question the story must always have a better answer to than silence.</li>
<li><strong>Classic System, double reading.</strong> Played fully straight for the Hero; through the elder's eyes it's just another institution, and this character has decades of opinions about institutions. Weariness isn't parody, so we can keep blue-box sincerity intact.</li>
</ul>
<p>I also noted the open questions the full concept will settle — chiefly <em>why</em> the elder is along for the ride (that choice is the emotional engine), whether they have a sheet and engage with the System (a rarely-glimpsed high-level stat block is catnip to RR readers), and what the slice-of-life home ground is between plot beats. Ready when you want to share the rest — or if it'd be useful first, I can write a trial scene with a placeholder version of this pairing to test the two-register voice.</p>
</div>


<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>Let's write a couple more trial scenes of different premises loosely matching this setup.</p>
<p>Some notes on your implications:</p>
<p>Never mock the Hero: I think a bit of mocking is allowed, but it has to be like... some mix of affectionate, exasperated, etc. The Hero is likely to be very annoying, and full of the foibles of youth, and the protagonist is allowed to be amused by that and poke fun at it, but they should never hold the hero <em>in contempt</em>.</p>
<p>Narration temper: Definitely. I think we can still have the protagonist get passionate about things, and definitely frustrated, but it's definitely not the fury of the Pratchett characters.</p>
<p>Power needs problems it can't solve: One of the ways we're going to achieve this is that the protagonist is extremely powerful but in ways completely unsuitable for most of the tasks actually at hand. He will occasionally be extremely useful, sometimes in ways that require lateral thinking on his or others' parts, but ends up as baggage or wandering off to do his own thing as often as not. Often his most useful power will be holding back the other characters from being an idiot.</p>
<p>Classic System, double reading: Yup. I think one of the things that will be going on in the story is that we'll very rarely see the protagonist getting levels, system messages, etc. He might e.g. get suprise levels in skills that his previous life neglected utterly, but he's very much not a numbers-go-up person. In contrast, the others will be doing classic levelling nonsense and the hero probably has some classically LitRPG broken skill of some sort.</p></blockquote>

The Hero is likely to be genuinely annoying and full of the foibles of<br />

<details><summary>contents of samples/round4/warcaster.md</summary>
<h1>Round four trial A — &quot;the warcaster&quot;</h1>
<p><strong>Loose premise:</strong> Aldous Crane, retired battle-mage. His entire art is<br />
army-scale: firestorms, mile-wide wards, weather. He has promised his late<br />
sister he'd look after her granddaughter Ivy, who has just been granted the<br />
[Chosen of the Dawn] class and a destiny, and who leads a bronze-rank party<br />
into dungeons that are, without exception, indoors.</p>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> third hour of a dungeon crawl.</p>
<hr />
<p>The dungeon was called the Halls of the Unquiet Dead, and it was eleven feet<br />
wide.</p>
<p>Aldous had measured it twice, out of a soldier's habit of confirming bad<br />
news. Eleven feet at the widest, seven and a half at the pinch points, with<br />
a ceiling he could touch without straightening his arm. His smallest<br />
practical working was the fire-fan he'd used at Redmarsh to close a cavalry<br />
lane. It required forty yards of clearance and consumed the air of roughly a<br />
barn. Casting it in here would kill everyone present, then pull the ceiling<br />
down on their corpses, and the party had asked him not to mention it again<br />
after the second time he'd worked this out loud.</p>
<p>So he carried the lantern. Somebody had to, and it kept his hands off things.</p>
<p>Up ahead, Ivy fought a bone-knight in the manner the Temple had trained her<br />
to, which was correct, square, and about a second and a half slower than it<br />
needed to be. The rest of them did their jobs around her. Pashet put arrows<br />
into the gaps. Brother Wick chanted. Tomas the rogue was somewhere in the<br />
shadows being, by his own account, essential.</p>
<p><em>[Dawn Blade] has reached Level 9,</em> announced the ceiling, more or less, in<br />
gold letters visible to everyone. The System liked an audience for Ivy. It<br />
had opinions about her destiny and shared them freely, like a proud aunt.</p>
<p>&quot;Nine!&quot; said Ivy, stepping over the settling bones. &quot;Uncle, did you see the<br />
riposte?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;I saw it.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;The instructor said I wouldn't get the riposte until twelve.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;The instructor hadn't met you.&quot; This was true in several directions at<br />
once, and Ivy took the good one, as she reliably did, and beamed, and went<br />
on up the corridor with her guard already dropping. Sixty years ago Aldous<br />
had watched recruits do exactly this between engagements. The ones who<br />
lived learned that a battle isn't over when you feel like telling someone<br />
about it. He hadn't found a way to teach that with words yet, in this<br />
company or any other, and had settled for walking close behind her with the<br />
lantern held high.</p>
<p>The corridor opened into a round chamber with a sarcophagus in the middle<br />
and coins all over the floor. The coins were arranged in a spiral. Tomas<br />
appeared out of nowhere with his eyes shining.</p>
<p>&quot;Gold,&quot; he said.</p>
<p>&quot;Pattern,&quot; said Aldous.</p>
<p>&quot;Trap,&quot; sighed Pashet, unshouldering her bow.</p>
<p>Ivy had already taken a step in. It was her class working; the [Chosen]<br />
package came with a pull toward the significant, and sarcophagi in round<br />
rooms were about as significant as furniture gets. Aldous took a handful of<br />
her cloak. Not dramatically. The way you take a kettle off the heat.</p>
<p>&quot;Wait for the count,&quot; he said.</p>
<p>&quot;There's nothing moving.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;There's nothing moving <em>yet</em>. Count the coins in the third ring.&quot;</p>
<p>She counted, mouthing it. The party waited. Wick sat down on his shield,<br />
which is what fifteen years of professional adventuring looks like when it<br />
gets the chance.</p>
<p>&quot;Thirteen,&quot; Ivy said. Then, slower: &quot;They're all thirteen.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;And what's the significance of thirteen down here?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Death magic. A summoning array.&quot; She looked at the spiral again, and he<br />
could watch the room reorganize itself in her head, treasure becoming<br />
diagram. &quot;It's not a hoard, it's a <em>sigil</em>. If I'd crossed it—&quot; She stopped<br />
herself, which was new this month, and pulled her foot back.</p>
<p>They went around by the wall, single file, while Tomas mourned quietly.<br />
Ivy disarmed the array from the far side with a Temple cancellation that<br />
was, Aldous had to admit, tidy work — his own solution to enemy arrays had<br />
always been to collapse the geology under them, which had cost the Crown a<br />
number of otherwise serviceable bridges.</p>
<p>At the far door he felt the small cold prickle at the base of his skull<br />
that meant the System wanted a private word. He knew what it would be.<br />
There were only ever two things it told him. It was not going to be his<br />
class, which had sat at its cap since before Ivy's mother was born, and<br />
which he had asked it to stop showing him.</p>
<p><em>[Lantern-Bearing] has reached Level 6.</em></p>
<p>He'd been dead certain that wasn't a real skill. He'd said so, in fact, at<br />
level three.</p>
<p>&quot;Anything?&quot; Ivy asked, glancing back. She always knew when it talked to him,<br />
and she was, on this one subject, unbearable.</p>
<p>&quot;Nothing,&quot; said Aldous. &quot;Mind the step.&quot;</p>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of samples/round4/parole.md</summary>
<h1>Round four trial B — &quot;the parole&quot;</h1>
<p><strong>Loose premise:</strong> Morvath, formerly the Undying Tyrant of the Ashen Throne,<br />
took the amnesty. Terms of parole: no dominion, no legions, and release into<br />
the custody of a licensed adventurer of good standing. The licensed<br />
adventurer of good standing is Bram, nineteen, [Bearer of the Sundered<br />
Crown], possessor of the broken skill [Rally] (all allies within earshot<br />
gain +40% to everything while Bram believes in the plan), and of the<br />
sincere conviction that Morvath is his party's &quot;support caster.&quot;</p>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> the Adventurers' Guild, posting hall, morning.</p>
<hr />
<p>The quest board at Greyford was managed by a clerk named Odd Sally, and the<br />
morning rush was governed by an etiquette that Bram's party had never once<br />
observed. You read the postings from the left. You did not call out reward<br />
numbers. You did not, under any circumstances, whoop.</p>
<p>&quot;Escort quest!&quot; whooped Bram. &quot;Silver-rank! Grain convoy to Pellsworth,<br />
twelve wagons, they want guards against the marsh raiders. Two hundred<br />
crowns!&quot;</p>
<p>The party gathered in. Morvath stayed where he was, by the hearth, drinking<br />
the Guild's terrible tea. Standing near notice boards gave him a specific<br />
discomfort he had never mentioned to anyone. He had governed through notice<br />
boards. An empire of forty provinces, and all of it, when you got down to<br />
the working parts, was pieces of paper nailed up where people had to stand<br />
and read what was going to happen to them next. He'd had better penmanship<br />
than this Guild, at least. His proclamations had been set in a typeface<br />
that made strong men sit down. This board used pins with little brass heads<br />
and someone had drawn a flower on the corner of the harvest-festival notice.</p>
<p>&quot;Two hundred crowns,&quot; Bram said again, arriving at the hearth with the<br />
posting in his hand and belief already coming off him like heat from a<br />
stove. Morvath could feel [Rally] reaching for him, checking, as it always<br />
did, whether he was an ally within earshot. The skill had never resolved<br />
the question. Neither had Morvath.</p>
<p>&quot;Read me the terms.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Twelve wagons, Greyford to Pellsworth by the levee road, seven days,<br />
payment on delivery, penalty clause for spoilage.&quot; Bram grinned. &quot;It's<br />
guarding <em>wagons</em>, Morvath. It basically pays itself.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Mm. Who's the client?&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Consortium of growers, it says. Care of a factor in Pellsworth.&quot;</p>
<p>Morvath held out his hand and read it himself. He read it the way he had<br />
read supply requisitions for two hundred years, which was assuming the<br />
author was lying and working backward to establish about what.</p>
<p>&quot;Grain moves to Pellsworth in the autumn,&quot; he said. &quot;This is spring. In<br />
spring, grain moves <em>from</em> Pellsworth, because the levee towns run short<br />
before first harvest and pay silly prices. Nobody guards twelve wagons of<br />
grain going the wrong direction at the wrong time of year.&quot; He turned the<br />
sheet over. No seal on the reverse. &quot;Also, the penalty clause is for<br />
spoilage, and grain doesn't spoil in seven days under tarpaulin. Fish<br />
spoils. Your growers' consortium is a smuggler moving something perishable<br />
in grain sacks, your two hundred crowns is because the marsh raiders are<br />
real and expected, and your factor in Pellsworth will develop a complaint<br />
about the cargo's condition and see the fee cut to fifty. I have employed<br />
this exact structure. I paid better.&quot;</p>
<p>There was a silence, into which the hearth popped.</p>
<p>&quot;So we shouldn't take it,&quot; said Lissa. She was the party's actual support<br />
caster, and had views about the fact.</p>
<p>&quot;Take it,&quot; said Morvath. &quot;Renegotiate. Payment half in advance, spoilage<br />
clause struck, and tell the posting agent you'll want to see the cargo<br />
loaded. If it's honest, they'll haggle and you'll get most of that. If it's<br />
what I think it is, they'll withdraw the posting within the hour rather<br />
than let you watch the loading, and you'll have lost nothing.&quot; He gave the<br />
sheet back. &quot;Either way you'll know by lunch.&quot;</p>
<p>Bram took the paper slowly. Morvath watched several futures move behind the<br />
boy's eyes: the crowns, the wagons, the version of this where he got to<br />
fight marsh raiders from a moving wagon, which would genuinely have suited<br />
his talents.</p>
<p>&quot;You're very good at this,&quot; Bram said, with the open admiration that<br />
Morvath, eight months into the arrangement, still had no procedure for.</p>
<p>&quot;I fed armies, boy.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Right, but as a <em>support</em>—&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Go and renegotiate.&quot;</p>
<p>They went, all five, in a cluster, [Rally] gathering them up so that they<br />
crossed the hall like one animal with excellent morale. Odd Sally caught<br />
Morvath's eye from behind the counter and mimed, with a weariness that<br />
crossed all former allegiances, the drawing of a flower on a proclamation.</p>
<p>Morvath finished the terrible tea. While he was setting the cup down, the<br />
System spoke to him for the first time in three weeks, in the same flat<br />
voice with which it had once announced his coronation and, later, the two<br />
relevant deaths.</p>
<p><em>[Haggling] has reached Level 4.</em></p>
<p>He considered filing an objection: he hadn't haggled, he had <em>advised</em>.<br />
He drafted a sentence of it and stopped, having caught, in the flatness of<br />
the blue, the faint suggestion that the System knew what a quartermaster's<br />
objection was worth, and had already survived a great many of them.</p>
<p>The posting came down off the board within the hour. Bram was disappointed<br />
about the raiders all afternoon, and cheered up at dinner, over a stew<br />
Morvath had corrected when nobody was looking.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of samples/round4/slow-tom.md</summary>
<h1>Round four trial C — &quot;Slow Tom&quot;</h1>
<p><strong>Loose premise:</strong> the being that wears the name Tom Underhill is, according<br />
to the oldest surviving treaty, &quot;the hill country, walking.&quot; His power works<br />
at the scale and speed of geology: he can raise a mountain range, given a<br />
few centuries and a reason. He owes the Brightwater family a debt from nine<br />
generations back, and the current Brightwater is Ness — guild-certified<br />
party leader, [Vanguard] class, keeper of a logistics notebook that has<br />
made grown quartermasters emotional — who has calculated the party's<br />
optimal marching order and cannot get Tom to stay in it.</p>
<p><strong>Scene:</strong> the road to Wyecombe, day four. An ambush.</p>
<hr />
<p>The hook-wolves hit the party at the ford, which was the correct place to<br />
hit them, and Ness had them formed up before the second howl. She'd drilled<br />
the formation on three rest days that the others had wanted to spend<br />
resting. It showed. Shields locked, spears out, casters behind, and the<br />
wolves came down the bank into a small, well-run wall and died against it<br />
in an orderly fashion, one against the current of the stream.</p>
<p>Tom watched from the far side of the ford, where he had been standing since<br />
somewhat before the attack began. He had not crossed with the others. Ness<br />
had a diagram about crossings, with his position marked on it in green ink,<br />
and he'd looked at the diagram with real admiration and then gone where he<br />
was going anyway, the way weather goes where it is going.</p>
<p>It wasn't that he wouldn't help. It was a question of what helping would<br />
mean. He could feel the hook-wolves the way you feel a fly land on your<br />
arm, and he could deal with them about as delicately: the options at his<br />
disposal began with the riverbed and went up from there. Forty years ago,<br />
the last time he'd intervened in a skirmish, the resulting landform had<br />
been named, mapped, and eventually consecrated, and the family whose farm<br />
it stood on had petitioned the Crown for relief. He had helped with the<br />
petition. It had seemed the least he could do, being the mountain in<br />
question.</p>
<p>So the party fought, and Tom stood in the shallows with the water going<br />
around his boots slightly wrong, and when the last wolf broke and ran up<br />
the bank, he was no longer there to watch it, having wandered off along the<br />
stream with his hands in his pockets.</p>
<p>Ness found him a quarter mile up, crouched at the waterline, one hand flat<br />
against a boulder like a doctor taking a pulse.</p>
<p>&quot;You <em>missed</em> it,&quot; she said. &quot;Formation worked. Textbook. Ollie made<br />
Level 11 and Dree got the last one at thirty yards, clean through the—&quot;<br />
She stopped and made herself breathe, because the notebook had a page about<br />
not narrating victories to Tom, who had watched the battle of Cold Marches<br />
from underneath it. &quot;You're touching a rock.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;I am.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;We're two hours behind the schedule.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;Mm.&quot; He straightened up, joints going off like a rope bridge. &quot;Ness. When<br />
did wolves last cross the Wye?&quot;</p>
<p>She had it in the notebook, in the fauna appendix, and it annoyed her that<br />
she checked, and it annoyed her more that he waited while she checked<br />
without a flicker of impatience, as though notebooks were a fine idea that<br />
would catch on any decade now. &quot;Never recorded south of Harding. They den<br />
in the high fells.&quot; She looked back down the stream, at the ford, at the<br />
angle of the banks. &quot;They were going the wrong way.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;They were going downhill,&quot; said Tom. &quot;Everything in the fells is going<br />
downhill this week. The stream's carrying fell-water it shouldn't have for<br />
a month yet, and it tastes of iron, and the stones,&quot; he patted the boulder,<br />
once, like an apology, &quot;are worried. Something's moved into the high<br />
country that's big enough to empty it.&quot;</p>
<p>Ness stood very still. Somewhere behind them, faint over the water, came<br />
the sound of Ollie being told the story of his own level-up by Dree, with<br />
sound effects.</p>
<p>&quot;The contract's for Wyecombe,&quot; she said, at last, in the voice of a woman<br />
already redrawing a route in her head. &quot;Escort only. High fells would be<br />
a new negotiation entirely.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;It would.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;You could <em>mention</em> these things at the planning stage.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;I didn't know it at the planning stage. The stones didn't know it till<br />
yesterday.&quot; He set off toward the ford at his only speed. &quot;You'd have found<br />
it in a day or two regardless. You're the sort that notices what's missing.<br />
It's a rarer skill than the other kind.&quot;</p>
<p>Ness wrote <em>iron / fell-water / worried(?)</em> in the margin of the fauna<br />
appendix, drew a box around <em>worried</em>, and could not immediately decide,<br />
looking at it, whether the box was a question or a fence.</p>
<p>By the time she'd caught him up he had wandered off the road again, ten<br />
yards, to stand in a field. As far as anyone could ever tell, standing in<br />
fields was most of what he did. It would be another three days before<br />
anyone understood what he'd been asking the field to do, and by then they<br />
would be very glad he'd asked.</p>
</details>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Committed: calibrations folded into <code>notes/premise.md</code>, and three trial scenes in <code>samples/round4/</code>, each testing a different flavor of the elder and a different position on the useful-to-baggage spectrum.</p>
<p><strong>A — <code>warcaster.md</code>.</strong> Aldous Crane, retired battle-mage whose smallest working needs forty yards of clearance, escorting his grandniece Ivy ([Chosen of the Dawn]) through a dungeon that is eleven feet wide. He carries the lantern. His useful act is grabbing her cloak before she walks onto a summoning array — and then making her do the reading herself rather than solving it for her. Surprise level: [Lantern-Bearing] 6. Tests the power-mismatch-indoors version and mentorship-by-question.</p>
<p><strong>B — <code>parole.md</code>.</strong> Morvath, ex-Dark Lord on parole in the custody of Bram, nineteen, whose broken skill [Rally] keeps pinging Morvath to check whether he counts as an ally — a question neither the skill nor Morvath has resolved, which I think is the kind of long-arc thread this structure wants. His usefulness is lateral: he reads a quest posting like a two-hundred-year-old requisitions officer and spots the smuggling scam from the seasonality of grain. Surprise level: [Haggling] 4, over his objection that he had <em>advised</em>. This one leans most into affectionate exasperation (&quot;Right, but as a <em>support</em>—&quot; / &quot;Go and renegotiate&quot;).</p>
<p><strong>C — <code>slow-tom.md</code>.</strong> Tom Underhill, &quot;the hill country, walking,&quot; whose intervention options start at the riverbed and go up — so during the ambush he does nothing at all, and the party's victory is entirely their own drilled competence. His contribution comes after: the wolves were going the wrong way, the stones are worried, something has emptied the high fells. He's the maximum-baggage, wanders-off-mid-scene version, and the scene ends on him standing in a field for reasons nobody will understand for three days.</p>
<p>Across all three I tried to hold the round-three discipline (plain sentences dominant, event-wit, ~one aphorism each, off-center-but-hooked endings) plus your calibrations: the Heroes are annoying in age-appropriate ways and genuinely good at their jobs — Ivy's tidy cancellation, Ness's drilled formation winning the fight — and the elders' fun-poking never lands as contempt. The variables worth your reaction: elder warmth (Aldous warmest, Morvath driest, Tom strangest), hero flavor (earnest knight, golden-retriever leader, competent planner), and how much the System intrudes on the elder's interiority.</p>
</div>


<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>One thing that I notice in two of these (parole and slow tom) is that they suffer from another LLM tic, which is the desire to wrap everything up in a tidy clever bit right at the end. This is sometimes fine, but is particularly bad for the daily cadence of chapters we're doing for. Take a look at some sample chapters from &quot;Chaotic Craftsman Worships the Cube&quot; (I've given you an epub) to get of a bit of a better idea of what the end of a chapter should look like. It's not quite daily update cadence, but it's quite frequent. Don't bother to read the whole thing, I enjoy this story but the writing style is mostly only OK.</p></blockquote>

<details><summary>contents of Bash command</summary>
<pre>cd "/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-drmaciver-Projects-autoroad/0c653b33-1efd-45bb-b7ab-26ed3dca981e/scratchpad/cube" &amp;&amp; python3 - &lt;&lt;'EOF'
import re, html, glob, os
files = sorted(glob.glob('OEBPS/file*.xhtml'))
print(len(files), 'chapters')
os.makedirs('endings', exist_ok=True)
import random
picks = files[1:4] + files[10:12] + files[25:27] + files[45:47] + files[-3:] if len(files)&gt;50 else files
out=open('endings/sample-endings.txt','w')
for f in picks:
    s = open(f, encoding='utf-8').read()
    title = re.search(r'&lt;title&gt;(.*?)&lt;/title&gt;', s)
    body = re.search(r'&lt;body[^&gt;]*&gt;(.*)&lt;/body&gt;', s, re.S).group(1)
    text = re.sub(r'&lt;/p&gt;', '\n\n', body)
    text = html.unescape(re.sub(r'&lt;[^&gt;]+&gt;', '', text))
    text = re.sub(r'\n{3,}', '\n\n', text).strip()
    paras = [p for p in text.split('\n\n') if p.strip()]
    out.write(f"===== {os.path.basename(f)} :: {title.group(1) if title else '?'} ({len(text.split())} words) =====\n")
    out.write('\n\n'.join(paras[-6:]))
    out.write('\n\n')
out.close()
print('wrote endings/sample-endings.txt')
EOF</pre>

    text = re.sub(r'&lt;/p&gt;', '\n\n', body)

<details><summary>contents of notes/voices.md</summary>
<p><em>old:</em></p>
<ol start="7">
<li><strong>Endings off-center, with a scale clause.</strong> Scenes end off-center — no<br />
callback buttons that click shut; end on the next piece of work, an<br />
unresolved noise, a departure handled badly. But a serial <em>chapter's</em><br />
final beat may click — cliffhanger, stinger, flash-forward — because the<br />
click is the retention mechanic. One narrator flash-forward per chapter<br />
maximum, and it counts as the aphorism slot.</li>
</ol>
<p><em>new:</em></p>
<ol start="7">
<li><strong>Endings are cuts, not cadences.</strong> The LLM tic runs deeper than callback<br />
buttons: it's the urge to end on ANY tidy clever bit — a resolving chord,<br />
an epigram, a flash-forward promise, a three-fold rhyme. At daily cadence<br />
this is poison: every chapter closing with a bow reads as 300 identical<br />
bows. The model (from Chaotic Craftsman Worships the Cube, a<br />
high-retention frequent-update serial): chapters <em>cut mid-flow</em> — on a<br />
decision made but not yet executed (&quot;Okay, screw that. I think I have an<br />
idea.&quot; / &quot;&lt;I already hate it.&gt;&quot;), mid-errand, or on the next task (&quot;I<br />
guess I'll get to work&quot;). The final line is usually plain, even flat; the<br />
pull into the next chapter is undone work, not crafted resonance. So:<br />
end where the reader's attention is pointing forward, then stop writing.<br />
Cleverness budget for the last two paragraphs of a chapter: near zero. A<br />
genuine cliffhanger or dread-beat is allowed when the story supplies one;<br />
a <em>composed</em> one is not. (Scene breaks inside chapters: same rule,<br />
gentler — off-center, on work or noise, no rhymes.)</li>
</ol>
</details>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Now the two endings. Parole first — cutting the meditative objection paragraph and the stew bow:</p>
</div>

<details><summary>contents of samples/round4/parole.md</summary>
<p><em>old:</em></p>
<p>Morvath finished the terrible tea. While he was setting the cup down, the<br />
System spoke to him for the first time in three weeks, in the same flat<br />
voice with which it had once announced his coronation and, later, the two<br />
relevant deaths.</p>
<p><em>[Haggling] has reached Level 4.</em></p>
<p>He considered filing an objection: he hadn't haggled, he had <em>advised</em>.<br />
He drafted a sentence of it and stopped, having caught, in the flatness of<br />
the blue, the faint suggestion that the System knew what a quartermaster's<br />
objection was worth, and had already survived a great many of them.</p>
<p>The posting came down off the board within the hour. Bram was disappointed<br />
about the raiders all afternoon, and cheered up at dinner, over a stew<br />
Morvath had corrected when nobody was looking.</p>
<p><em>new:</em></p>
<p>While he was setting the cup down, the System spoke to him for the first<br />
time in three weeks, in the same flat voice with which it had once<br />
announced his coronation and, later, the two relevant deaths.</p>
<p><em>[Haggling] has reached Level 4.</em></p>
<p>He hadn't haggled. He had advised. He began composing the objection, got<br />
one sentence in, and abandoned it, because across the hall the posting<br />
agent had just seen who was walking toward his desk with a grain contract<br />
and a party of five, and had started tidying his papers in the manner of a<br />
man who suddenly remembers an appointment elsewhere.</p>
<p>Morvath left the tea and went after them. If the posting was coming down,<br />
he wanted to see which door the client's man used.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>contents of samples/round4/slow-tom.md</summary>
<p><em>old:</em></p>
<p>Ness wrote <em>iron / fell-water / worried(?)</em> in the margin of the fauna<br />
appendix, drew a box around <em>worried</em>, and could not immediately decide,<br />
looking at it, whether the box was a question or a fence.</p>
<p>By the time she'd caught him up he had wandered off the road again, ten<br />
yards, to stand in a field. As far as anyone could ever tell, standing in<br />
fields was most of what he did. It would be another three days before<br />
anyone understood what he'd been asking the field to do, and by then they<br />
would be very glad he'd asked.</p>
<p><em>new:</em></p>
<p>Ness wrote <em>iron / fell-water / worried(?)</em> in the margin of the fauna<br />
appendix and looked at it for a while.</p>
<p>By the time she caught him up he had wandered off the road again, ten<br />
yards into a fallow field, and was standing in it. She gave that thirty<br />
seconds, established that no explanation was coming, and got out the route<br />
map instead. If they pushed past the ford tonight they could reach Harding<br />
by noon tomorrow. Harding would have fell-shepherds in it, and<br />
fell-shepherds would know what had come down out of the high country, and<br />
the client was going to have to be written to either way.</p>
<p>&quot;Tom. We're leaving.&quot;</p>
<p>&quot;In a minute,&quot; said Tom, to her, or possibly to the field.</p>
</details>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Done and committed. Glad the scenes otherwise land — I've left everything but the endings untouched.</p>
<p>What I took from the Cube sample (I pulled the closing paragraphs from eleven chapters spread across its 1,098): its chapters don't end, they <em>cut</em>. The last beat is a decision made but not executed (&quot;Okay, screw that. I think I have an idea.&quot; / &quot;&lt;I already hate it.&gt;&quot;), an errand in progress (carrying quartz back to the shop, missing his proper tools the whole way), or simply the next task (&quot;Cool, in that case, I guess I'll get to work&quot;). The final line is plain, often flat, and the pull into the next chapter is undone work rather than crafted resonance. Which reframes the earlier &quot;chapter finals may click&quot; clause I'd taken from Avaunt — Avaunt is a complete novel and can afford chords; at frequent-update cadence, three hundred bows in a row is its own uniformity tell. The voice rule now reads: end where the reader's attention points forward, then stop writing; cleverness budget for a chapter's last two paragraphs is near zero; a cliffhanger is allowed when the story genuinely supplies one, a <em>composed</em> one is not.</p>
<p>The two fixes:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Parole</strong> previously closed with a triple bow (posting comes down, Bram sulks, secretly-corrected stew). Now the [Haggling] ding interrupts itself — Morvath abandons his objection mid-sentence because across the hall the posting agent has spotted who's approaching and started tidying his papers — and it cuts on Morvath following to see which door the client's man uses.</li>
<li><strong>Slow Tom</strong> previously closed on a narrator flash-forward promise (&quot;by then they would be very glad he'd asked&quot;) — exactly the composed-cliffhanger move. Now Ness gets thirty seconds of no explanation, opens the route map, starts replanning for Harding, and it cuts on &quot;In a minute,&quot; said Tom, to her, or possibly to the field.</li>
</ul>
<p>The mystery of the field still hangs there doing its forward-pull work; it just isn't gift-wrapped anymore.</p>
</div>

Findings are quote + canon citation + contradiction/drift/ambiguity classification + minimal fix, never silently applied. Second mandatory half: extract newly established facts back into the canon files, so canon grows with each accepted chapter rather than being reconstructed later.</li>
<li><strong><code>editing-pass</code></strong> — two passes. Pass 1 is the hostile-literal-reader sense check, with your &quot;need me gone&quot; catch recorded as the canonical example of inverted intent, plus checks like verifying every question against the answer it receives (mismatches localize errors). Pass 2 is mechanical voice compliance: em-dash counting, the banned-construction list, aphorism census, prose-at-rest runs, endings-cut enforcement, tone calibration, and the jukebox test on every flourish. Sense findings are always fix-worthy; voice findings are flagged as overrulable recommendations.</li>
</ul>
<p>One suggestion for when we build the chapter workflow proper: the consistency-check's canon-maintenance half should probably run as its own stage <em>after</em> a draft is accepted rather than during review, so a rejected draft never pollutes canon. The skill currently notes the ordering but the workflow will make it structural.</p>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>

<ul>
<li>You can level up skills, attributes, and class separately, and they all respond to hard work but generally classes are levelled up by achievements (including levelling related skills), attributes are levelled up by using them, and skills are levelled up mostly by learning and practicing. <li>Skills are a sort of &quot;training wheel&quot;. They point out and (if you let them) correct errors, nudge you with knowledge that you need and can be expected to have at that level to sort of fill in the gaps, and point you in the right direction to observe new and interesting things. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> One of the reasons Edwin is as good as he is is that he relentlessly works towards &quot;filling in&quot; every level of the skill to the absolute best of your ability.</li>
<li>When you level up a class it grants you bonuses to stats, improves your abilities, etc. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></li>
<li>Classes evolve / cap out at particular levels. Maybe every 100 levels you tier up and have to select a new class? Edwin should have quite a few tier-ups under his belt and be about halfway through his current one. He will probably not get more than one or two levels in it over the course of the entire story.</li>
<li>There might be some sort of experience tracked internally, but it's not visible to the users of the system.</li>
<li>There should be some sort of achievement system. This doesn't always do anything visible.</li>
</ul></blockquote>

<p>Done and committed. Three pieces:</p>
<p><strong>The skill</strong> (<code>.claude/skills/consistency-backfill/SKILL.md</code>) — the two-way negotiation you described: extract what a chapter <em>implies</em> beyond what it states (including implications of absence — nobody reacting to something means it's normal), then reconcile in whichever direction is cheaper. Key design choices: a three-level claim taxonomy every canon file uses (<strong>Established</strong> = on-page and cited, <strong>Working</strong> = invented during backfill, binding but cheap to change while nothing depends on it, <strong>Open questions</strong> = explicitly undecided, with drafts forbidden from resolving them casually); a separate &quot;corner-watch&quot; step that flags commitments that would constrain future story even when they're internally consistent, because &quot;is it consistent&quot; and &quot;do we want to be stuck with it&quot; are different questions; and a lazy-growth rule — canon stays one step ahead of need, stubs appearing when an entity starts mattering. I also trimmed consistency-check's overlapping canon-maintenance section so the pipeline is clean: consistency-check → editing-pass → consistency-backfill.</p>
<p><strong>The restructure</strong> — <code>notes/canon/</code> is now a directory tree (<code>characters/</code>, <code>world/</code>, plus <code>system.md</code> and <code>deferred-beats.md</code>), with relative links between files, and both existing skills updated to the new layout.</p>
<p><strong>The initial backfill</strong> — I ran the skill's procedure on chapters 1–2 as its first live test. Eleven files now exist: bios for Edwin, Peri, and Sorrel plus a <code>minor.md</code> holding Dob, Mrs. Hobbet, the Fenholt farmer, the storm-caller, and the rest; world files for geography (with travel arithmetic: ~20 miles/day walking, derived from Fenholt), magic-as-craft, institutions, the green revolution, and a day-by-day timeline pinning the story to late summer with the Seal cooldown ending Day 30.</p>
<p>Things the backfill surfaced that are worth your attention:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Edwin's obscurity is load-bearing.</strong> Chapter 1 requires that Peri had never heard of him and needed the Weave. So whatever fame the green revolution bought him must be the wrong kind — agricultural, dispersed, spells known without an author attached. Recorded as an open question with that constraint attached.</li>
<li><strong>Mage longevity</strong> is now a flagged do-not-resolve-casually question: whatever explains 350 years touches demography, his biography, and possibly the plot.</li>
<li><strong>&quot;Six kings and two church synods&quot;</strong> (ch. 2 hyperbole about moving him) quietly established kings-plural and organized synods in living memory — exactly the kind of accidental commitment the corner-watch exists to catch. I kept it, since it's cheap, but it's in <code>institutions.md</code> now rather than lurking.</li>
<li><strong>No adventurers' guild exists on any page yet.</strong> The round-4 samples had one, but those are explicitly non-canon; I've noted the decision as open so the classic-LitRPG frame doesn't smuggle it in unexamined.</li>
</ul>
</div>

<p><strong>Why still medieval after 10,000+ System years?</strong> The System itself is the answer, and pleasingly, the mechanisms are already in canon. Skills replace institutions — each person gets a private tutor, so the machinery of impersonal knowledge (the thing that compounds into science) never needed building. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Power accumulates in levels rather than tools — nobody funds a cannon works when tier-4 battle mages exist and cannons don't level you. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> His fill-in method is the rare crack in the homogenization brake, and his revolution worked because he did the second un-System thing too: built an institution (courses, taught teachers, the college) rather than just a skill.</p>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>I'd like to build out a general authorship structure for how this should be written.</p>
<p>The structure is as follows:</p>
<p>The story is divided into books. Each book is divided into 2-4 arcs. Arcs are 30-50k words with a related starting and stopping point (e.g. a visit to a particular town might be an arc, even if several things happen in that town). Arcs should be broken up into chapters of 3k-5k words (it's OK for them to be longer if there really isn't a natural stopping point before 5k. The hard cut off is at 8k, but that should rarely be exceeded). We're aiming to publish one chapter per day.</p>
<p>We should have a general idea at all times of what the current book is about, and a relatively detailed plan for what happens each arc in that book. These may be revised as we write, especially if we find something more interesting or run into inconsistencies in the plan, but they're the starting point. We should apply the world and character consistency and backfill checks at this point as well as in individual chapters.</p>
<p>We should keep summaries of previous arcs and books.</p>
<p>We should generally write an arc at a time, with a workflow that writes the whole arc (possibly delegating each chapter to subagents, not sure, experiment) and then runs a revision pass over the whole work. This might not be quite right though - we might e.g. want to write a couple chapters and critique them before we write the rest. To experiment.</p>
<p>There should be a skill for managing all of this, and also a more specific skill for writing an arc.</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence</span> Don't use the existing chapter material - delete that and start again from scratch.</p></blockquote>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>By the way, we're going to want to get to a point where I'm &quot;just a normal reader&quot; (though I'll give you feedback - not decisions - as I read), so I'm not going to read too far ahead in the plans, and I'd like us to get to a point where I don't need to make these sorts of decisions for you. I think generally I'll try to stay a book ahead of Royal Road so we don't ever write ourselves into a corner, but I'd like to get to the point where you can author a full book confidently without asking me to decide anything.</p>
<p>Can we have a high level discussion about how to make decisions? Not necessarily the same ones I would, but ones that I would approve of (even if I disagred with them!)</p></blockquote>


<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Yes — and I think this session actually generated good data for it, because you just made twelve decisions against my recommendations, and the pattern of where you diverged is more informative than the decisions themselves.</p>
<p><strong>What the divergences reveal.</strong> Nine of the twelve you accepted roughly as recommended. The redirections were: the ch. 016 cost (I offered a menu of deaths and injuries; you chose relationship damage), Peri's box (I defaulted to the fate-magic ding; you gave her a sword level and made fate magic slow and useless early), and the leveling speed (I recommended conservative; you said fast is fine, tier-0 class). Each redirection has a legible principle behind it:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Emotional stakes come from relationships and work, not from violence.</strong> I reached for the standard dramatic currency (who gets hurt); you reached for what the event <em>does to how two people see each other</em> — which is also the choice that feeds the story's actual spine. Generalization: when choosing a cost, ask what it changes in a relationship, not what it destroys.</li>
<li><strong>The special thing must not be the competence engine.</strong> Peri's unique class is her <em>situation</em>, not her power curve; her earned growth is mundane (drills, a sword). That's the competence-porn rule applied to progression design.</li>
<li><strong>Prefer the choice that makes earlier design load-bearing.</strong> Your delving ruling reused the training-wheels theory rather than adding a dungeon-XP mechanic. Your death-ending interest was partly <em>because</em> the aging canon would become retroactively structural. When a question arises, the first move should be &quot;what does the existing design already imply?&quot; — a new mechanism is a last resort.</li>
<li><strong>The world is coherent before it is dramatic.</strong> Resentment of the green revolution: you declined a live conflict that 300 years of prosperity wouldn't actually sustain, and kept the residue (old mages' snobbery) that it would. No tension is kept alive just because stories usually want one.</li>
<li><strong>Don't fill the genre slot; don't subvert it either.</strong> <span class="redacted">redacted</span> is the obvious romance candidate and instead becomes the big sister — but Peri's side of the genre stays played fully straight. The rule isn't contrarianism; it's that every element earns its role from function, and the <em>reader-facing</em> genre contract is kept honestly (your tier-up pacing concern was a reader-expectation concern, not a mechanics one).</li>
<li><span class="redacted">redacted sentence</span> When options are close, the one that forecloses less wins — and a genuinely irreversible commitment (a first Weave quote, a named cost) is worth stopping to think about even when the choice itself seems small.</li>
</ol>
<p><strong>What &quot;approvable, not identical&quot; means operationally.</strong> I think your bar, stated from the outside, is: the decision is <em>derivable</em> — someone reading the canon, theme, and these principles could reconstruct why it was made; it's <em>coherent</em> — it doesn't fight anything already established or quietly resolve something marked open; and it's <em>cheap to overrule</em> — flagged, attributed, and not welded into ten chapters before you see it. A decision you disagree with but approve of is one where you can see the reasoning, the reasoning comes from your own recorded principles, and undoing it costs a paragraph, not an arc. The failures you'd rightly not approve are the ones where I decided by genre reflex (my cost menu), by convenience, or silently.</p>
<p><strong>How I'd get there mechanically:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>A decision-principles file</strong> (<code>notes/decision-principles.md</code>), seeded with the list above, grown by backfill exactly like canon — every ruling you make gets mined for its <em>revealed principle</em>, not just its content. Crucially, this continues working after you become a reader: reader feedback (&quot;this chapter felt off&quot;) gets the same treatment — I diagnose the violated principle, propose the generalization, record it flagged.</li>
<li><strong>Shrink the ask-list by category, not all at once.</strong> Right now: worldbuilding texture and mechanics derivations I should just decide (flagged). <span class="redacted">redacted sentence</span></li>
<li><strong>Calibration by shadow decisions.</strong> For the remaining book-1 decisions, I decide <em>first</em>, in writing, then ask — and we track the divergence. When my shadow decisions stop diverging in ways you care about, the asking stops. This session's twelve were accidentally the first round of exactly that, and 9/12-with-legible-misses is a decent baseline.</li>
<li><strong>Adversarial self-review at decision time.</strong> For anything with a long shadow, the same trick as the editing pipeline: a critique pass whose rubric is the principles file and whose question is not &quot;is this good?&quot; but &quot;would DRMacIver approve of how this was decided?&quot; — checking derivability, coherence, and reversibility, not taste.</li>
</ul>
<p>One honest caveat: principle 6 has a tension with your goal. Staying reversible-by-default is how I avoid needing you, but a book has to <em>spend</em> commitments to be any good — the cost chapter works because it doesn't mend. So the principle can't be &quot;never commit&quot;; it has to be &quot;commit knowingly, at the right blast radius, with the reasoning on record.&quot; That judgment — which commitments are worth their irreversibility — is the genuinely hard part, and I'd expect it to be the last thing the shadow-decision calibration gets right.</p>
<p>Does this match how you'd describe your own bar? And two things worth your input while we're here: anything you'd add to or strike from the principles list, and whether the &quot;structural commitments stay yours through book 1&quot; line is drawn where you'd draw it.</p>
</div>

Because I'm reading a book ahead if I think you do anything absolutely disastrous, we can fix that before it hits RR.</p>
