<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>question: given token caching, would it be more efficient to just write the entire arc in one agent rather than repeatedly resummarizing?</p></blockquote>


<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Yes, almost certainly — and it's a bigger saving than the lens redesign. The arithmetic:</p>
<p><strong>Current design</strong>: each of 11 drafters reads the full doc set plus every prior chapter fresh — roughly 60k tokens of docs at chapter 1 growing to ~120k by chapter 11, all at full input price, since file contents arrive as tool results in different positions and don't share cache across agents. Order of 1M full-price input tokens per arc, most of it the same text read eleven times.</p>
<p><strong>Single persistent agent</strong>: the docs and the growing arc sit in one context; each new chapter pays cache-read price (~10%) on the prefix plus full price only on the new material. Same arc costs roughly 100–150k full-price-equivalent input — a 5–10× reduction on the drafting side, and it also removes the &quot;each drafter calibrates on whatever it happened to read&quot; variance.</p>
<p>Two real costs to design around:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Long-context degradation</strong>: by chapter 11 the agent is at ~150k tokens of context, and drafting quality tends to sag well before that. Mitigation: bound it — one agent per half-arc (~5–6 chapters, ~90k context), with the second half's agent reading the first half from disk once. Still 2 reads instead of 11.</li>
<li><strong>The lead gate</strong>: the gate has to interrupt drafting after two chapters, and Workflow <code>agent()</code> calls are one-shot. But a persistent Agent continued via SendMessage keeps its context and cache across the interruption — draft two chapters, stop, run the gate (Sonnet lenses + Fable adjudicate, cheap and parallel in a Workflow), then message the same drafter with the verdict and &quot;continue.&quot; That's the most efficient shape of all: the drafter never re-reads anything.</li>
</ol>
<p>Same logic already applies to the edit stage — the single Fable synthesizer <em>is</em> the one-reader design — and to the lenses, which are cheap precisely because each reads little.</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence</span></p>
</div>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>Let's work a bit on the farm facts, because I don't think they're quite right now. This is partly content, partly authorial voice.</p>
<p>First off, content: We shouldn't explain spells or world-building in the author's notes. This is a place for real farm facts that are grounded in real world farming knowledge. Ideally I'd also like it spaced out a bit better (it's fine if this leaves some things unexplained if they're not super important) so there aren't giant infodumps about 5 different things in a chapter. One giant infodump about one thing is fine, a couple of short sentences about several things is fine, combining one infodump with a few sentencs about other things is also fine, but if it's getting long then maybe some can be deferred to the second time a topic comes up.</p>
<p>Secondly, author voice. I think right now it's boring. I think our imagined author should be someone with something of a farming special interest (probably not an actual farmer, maybe an agriculture student? They've spent time on a farm but they're more of a scientist than a farmer) who is excited to share Fun! Farm! Facts! with you and mostly keeps it about context that is needed to understand story details but occasionally can't resist going off on a digression. The voice should be consistent with them being the author of the rest of the story, but not necessarily in the style and register that you'd have for the narrator. I think our fictional author should also have a character bio that we try to keep consistent with.</p></blockquote>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>I find a lot of the dialogue in this novel a bit off in a way that I can't quite put my finger on. It feels like the characters aren't quite having a conversation like people, but are instead just saying things at each other. Often there's a line that is probably very clever but that doesn't actually quite make enough sense left to dangle where the recipient would normally be confused and say as such (many of those lines are just bad comms that could be fixed, some are probably genuinely better if the recipient is confused). There are also answers to questions that don't really explain much, or feel like they're answering the wrong question.</p>
<p>Can you find a couple of dialogue samples and we'll talk about what makes them work or not work?</p></blockquote>


<div class="assistant-prose">

</details>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Here are five exchanges from chapters 2, 12 and 19, chosen to cover the different failure types you describe, plus one that I think works, for contrast.</p>
<p><strong>1. The dangling clever line, then a scene break</strong> — <code>chapters/012-the-charter-board.md:226-232</code></p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> &quot;<span class="redacted">redacted</span> and his brother. Stood at F for a while. Neither of them knocked.&quot;</p>
<blockquote>
<h2>&quot;Did they want anything?&quot;<br />
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></h2>
</blockquote>
<p>Edwin asks a plain question and gets an epigram. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Knocking? Being watched? Edwin would ask. Instead the scene cuts, which is the tell: the line was too good to follow and too vague to answer, so it's parked where nobody has to respond. Mattie also sounds exactly like Edwin here; that's a second problem I'll come back to.</p>
<p><strong>2. The answer that's a shape, not an answer</strong> — <code>012:218-222</code></p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<blockquote>
<p>&quot;What's the difference?&quot;<br />
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
</blockquote>
<p>Peri asks a real, practical question: how do I word this? The reply is a balanced antithesis about <em>consequences</em>, which tells her nothing about how to write the sentence. It has the rhythm of wisdom and the content of a fortune cookie. A person answering would say something like &quot;Say the field's too small for the line, not that the neighbours won't let you on&quot; — and then, if he must, the epigram. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<p><strong>3. The echo</strong> — <code>019:499-503</code>, and everywhere</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted</span>.<br />
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br />
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> It's true of <span class="redacted">redacted</span>.&quot; / &quot;It's true of <span class="redacted">redacted</span>.&quot;). One speaker repeats the other's line verbatim, then somebody adds a swerve. Once a book it's a beat; this often, it's a metronome, and it's the single biggest reason the dialogue feels like people delivering lines rather than talking — nobody ever repeats your sentence back at you in a conversation unless they're stalling or mocking. Bonus in the <span class="redacted">redacted</span> one: &quot;That's your sentence&quot; refers to something Edwin said in an earlier chapter, so the reader gets an echo of a line they can't place.</p>
<p><strong>4. The mirror retort</strong> — <code>012:244-246</code></p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br />
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
<p>Edwin answers by reusing her sentence frame and flipping the ending. It's a cousin of the echo: the reply is generated from the <em>form</em> of the previous line rather than from what Edwin thinks. Real people occasionally do this when scoring a point; here it's the default way disagreement gets expressed. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted</span> is steamrolling <span class="redacted">redacted</span> and the non-answer is the point, and <span class="redacted">redacted</span> visibly loses.</p>
<p><strong>5. The oblique answer that works</strong> — <code>002:161-163</code></p>
<blockquote>
<p>&quot;Well?&quot; said Edwin.<br />
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>
</blockquote>
<p>Superficially the same fault as #1: she answers a different question. But it's earned: the wrists have been set up all chapter as the thing Edwin actually judges people by, Sorrel is being deliberately withholding and both of them know it, and Edwin's silence afterwards reads as him getting it. The recipient isn't confused; the reader isn't confused; the obliqueness is characterisation, not evasion by the author. Similar: <code>019:451-453</code> — Edwin says &quot;It's alive. All of it's alive. That's not that,&quot; which is opaque, and <span class="redacted">redacted</span> immediately says &quot;Then say what it is,&quot; and he does. Opaque line + recipient calls it + speaker clarifies is the honest version of the pattern.</p>
<p><strong>What I think the underlying thing is.</strong> Two causes stacking:</p>
<ul>
<li>Lines are generated from the previous line's <em>wording</em> (echo, mirror, antithesis) rather than from the speaker's goal in the exchange. That produces exchanges that are locally witty and globally not a conversation — nobody is trying to get anything from anybody.</li>
<li>Everyone speaks in the narrator's register. Mattie, <span class="redacted">redacted</span>, Sorrel, <span class="redacted">redacted</span>, <span class="redacted">redacted</span> all produce the same clipped, balanced, aphoristic sentence Edwin does. So even when a line is fine, the <em>turn-taking</em> feels like one mind talking to itself. <span class="redacted">redacted</span> at <code>012:378-386</code> is the strongest counterexample — she gets a long, un-tidy run with numbers and repetition and a grudge in it, and she sounds like a person.</li>
</ul>
<p>I'd also flag that <code>notes/voices.md</code> already bans most of this for narration (negative parallelism, triads with a swerve, ≤1 aphorism per scene) but I don't think the rules are being applied <em>inside quotation marks</em>, which is where they've migrated to.</p>
<p>Happy to go through any of these more closely, or pull samples from other chapters if you want to check whether the pattern holds across the book before we talk about rules.</p>
</div>


<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong></p>
<p>I think these are pretty good examples. Suggest some rules?</p></blockquote>


<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Here's a proposed set. They're written to slot into <code>notes/voices.md</code> as a &quot;Dialogue&quot; section, with the same binding force as the narration rules.</p>
<p><strong>1. Every line has a want.</strong> Before a character speaks, the writer should be able to say what they're trying to get from the other person with this line (an answer, agreement, to close the subject, to make them feel something). A line whose only purpose is to be a good line is cut. Test: could you paraphrase the line as an instruction to the listener?</p>
<p><strong>2. Answer the question that was asked, then be clever if you must.</strong> If the question is practical (&quot;what's the difference?&quot;, &quot;did they want anything?&quot;), the first sentence of the reply answers it in usable terms. Epigrams may follow; they may not substitute. Exception: the speaker is <em>deliberately</em> not answering, in which case rule 4 applies.</p>
<p><strong>3. Narration bans apply inside quotation marks.</strong> Negative parallelism, triads with a swerve, aphorism budget (≤1 per scene, <em>total</em>, narration and dialogue combined), em-dash budget, house words. Dialogue has been the place these constructions went to survive.</p>
<p><strong>4. Obliqueness must be paid for on the page.</strong> If a line would leave its listener confused, one of three things happens next: the listener says so and the speaker clarifies; the listener visibly gets it and the reader has been given enough to get it too; or the speaker is being evasive and the evasion is itself the point and is treated as such. What may not happen: a scene break, a topic change, or a stage direction (&quot;she shifted the chain&quot;) so the line goes unanswered.</p>
<p><strong>5. No echoes.</strong> A character does not repeat the previous speaker's sentence verbatim or near-verbatim as their reply. Budget: at most one deliberate echo per <em>arc</em>, and it must be doing something a plain reply can't (mockery, shock, ritual). &quot;The rings.&quot; / &quot;The rings.&quot; is banned outright.</p>
<p><strong>6. No mirror retorts.</strong> A reply may not be built by reusing the previous line's sentence frame with the ending swapped. If a character disagrees, they say what they think in their own construction.</p>
<ol start="7">
<li><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Everyone else gets a distinguishable habit, written down in their character file and checked against: <span class="redacted">redacted</span> runs long, numbers, repeats herself when angry, doesn't finish the tidy way; Mattie is short and concrete and never generalises; <span class="redacted">redacted</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Test: strip the tags from a page, and a reader should be able to reattach most of them.</li>
</ol>
<p><strong>8. People don't listen perfectly.</strong> In at least some exchanges per chapter someone mishears, answers the wrong part, interrupts, changes subject, or has to be told twice. Currently every speaker receives every line in full and replies to its best reading. Conversations should be a little lossy.</p>
<p><strong>9. Back-references must be reader-legible.</strong> A line that quotes something said in an earlier chapter (&quot;That's your sentence&quot;) is either re-anchored on the page (&quot;what you said at Mattie's table, that they take a dry line&quot;) or cut.</p>
<p><strong>10. Cut the last clever line of an exchange.</strong> Exchanges, like chapters, end on work, not cadence. If the final line of a dialogue run is the wittiest, that's usually the one that's dangling; move it up or drop it.</p>
<p>Suggested pipeline changes: add <code>dialogue: line-without-want</code>, <code>dialogue: echo</code>, <code>dialogue: mirror</code>, <code>dialogue: unanswered-question</code>, <code>dialogue: wrong-register</code> to the editing-pass finding types, and add a strip-the-tags check (rule 7) to the consistency check.</p>
<p>The two I'd expect to do most of the work are 1 and 7; the rest are mostly mechanical enforcement of those two. If you're happy with them I'll write them into <code>notes/voices.md</code>, add the finding types to the editing-pass skill, and put per-speaker grammar into the character files.</p>
</div>
